 1

 Demonstration Test Report:
  Vacuum Thermal Desorption (VTD) Testing to Support West Area Waste Feed Test 2

 Executive Summary

Approximately 54 million gallons of radioactive and hazardous waste are stored in underground
storage tanks at the U.S. Department of Energy’s (DOE’s) Hanford Site in southeastern
Washington State. The Hanford Waste Treatment and Immobilization Plant (WTP) is being
constructed to immobilize the waste in glass waste forms. The baseline configuration of the WTP
includes a Pretreatment Facility to separate the wastes into a small volume of high-level waste
(HLW) containing most of the radioactivity and a larger volume of low-activity waste (LAW)
containing most of the non-radioactive chemicals. The HLW will be converted to glass in the HLW
vitrification facility for ultimate disposal at a federal repository.

DOE has established the Direct Feed Low Activity Waste (DFLAW) program where Hanford tank
waste is pretreated through the Tank Side Cesium Removal (TSCR) system. The Hanford LAW
pretreated at TSCR is transferred under DFLAW directly to the WTP LAW Facility for
vitrification. The resulting glass waste form is then planned for disposal onsite at the Integrated
Disposal Facility (IDF). Using currently approved process models, the LAW WTP Facility is not
expected to have the capacity to immobilize all of the LAW. This results in a portion of the LAW
inventory requiring an alternate immobilization strategy (ORP-11242).

The need to develop an alternate immobilization strategy to address Land Disposal Restriction
(LDR) constituents in a cementitious waste form made from Hanford LAW was highlighted in
both the 2018 and 2023 Federally Funded Research and Development Centers (FFRDC) reports
provided to congressionally mandated National Academy of Science (NAS) studies on methods
for treating and disposing of Hanford LAW (SRNL-RP-2018-00687, SRNL-STI-2023-00007).
Furthermore, in 2021 the DOE Hanford Field office issued direction to the tank operations
contractor (Washington River Protection Solutions, LLC, [WRPS] at the time, now Hanford Tank
Waste Operations & Closure, [H2C]) requesting an Analysis of Alternatives (AoA) be developed
to address the need to “ …mitigate risks and add operational capabilities to enable near term
retrievals, treat tank waste in the 200 West Area Tank Farms, and manage DST space.” The AoA
was subsequently released in January 2022 and ultimately resulted in the West Area Tank
Treatment (WATT) Program. This program is currently preparing the 200 West Area to support
tank waste retrieval, pretreatment to remove solids and key radionuclides to the maximum extent
practical and proposed commercial treatment of liquid pretreated tank waste (PTW; chemically
very similar to LAW) for final treatment and out-of-state disposal as a grouted cementitious
solidified/stabilized waste form.
 2

Alternate treatment of LAW to address LDR organics through TD*X Associates (TD*X) vacuum
thermal desorption (VTD) process is an effective and lower cost option when compared to the
baseline vitrification process. Furthermore, the VTD process is established and currently permitted
at the EnergySolutions Clive Facility to treat organic-contaminated waste at less than Class A
radioactive concentrations. Removing LDR organics through VTD will aid in meeting or
exceeding project milestones at Hanford. Results of this demonstration and the prior demonstration
test in August 2024 confirm that the VTD system removes LDR organics to below LDR treatment
standards.

An unknown portion of Hanford LAW/PTW is predicted to contain organic chemical constituents
that are above the RCRA LDR treatment standards. The waste containing LDR organics will
require treatment through removal or destruction technologies as part of the treatment plan. The
ability of the VTD process to remove organics has been clearly demonstrated through two
successful demonstration tests of spiked Hanford LAW/PTW simulant waste; as well as over 21
years of successful Low Level Mixed Waste (LLMW) treatment of wastes from essentially every
DOE facility.

Target representative organic and inorganic chemicals were spiked at conservatively high
concentrations into a Hanford LAW simulant waste matrix. Each of these constituents of concern
were removed to below LDR treatment standards in the treated residual desorber solids with all
results being non-detectable. Also, condensate recovered during the demonstration test meets
established disposal criteria and can be transported and disposed under the existing TD*X
Associates license to commercial disposal facilities.

Removal efficiencies for constituents of concern confirm that even higher organic concentrations
could be removed to less than LDR thresholds, as demonstrated over the past 21 years by
EnergySolutions and TD*X in Clive, UT. Together EnergySolutions and TD*X have performed
five demonstration tests for RCRA and TSCA compliance with a wide range of organic
contaminants including polychlorinated biphenyls (PCB’s) at concentrations of up to 656,000 ppm
including the highest boiling point mixture (Arochlor 1260), resulting in non-detectible levels of
PCB’s in the treated solid residuals (<0.087 mg/kg to <0.481 mg/kg). Removal efficiencies for
these tests have been confirmed at greater than 99.9999%. These significant technical results
support the use of the TD*X VTD unit for materials like the Hanford LAW/PTW. The boiling
point range of PCBs is from 527oF to 842oF, which is representative of the highest boiling point
range for Hanford LAW/PTW contaminants, and much higher than many of the numerous
potential organic chemical contaminants in that material.

 3

Data and results from this testing report are intended to support the development of a Sample-and-
Send approach for Hanford PTW. The strategy includes sampling retrieved PTW for LDR
constituents and radionuclides used in 10 CFR 61.55 waste classification, transferring the waste
stream to pretreatment on the Hanford site to remove solids and key radionuclides, and when
required, based on the presence of LDR organics, the waste stream is processed by a demonstrated
removal or destruction treatment technology such as TD*X VTD. The purpose of this second
round of testing was to confirm that selected regulated compounds that bound the boiling points
of the 132 reasonably expected to be present LDR organics associated with Hanford tank waste
can be effectively treated by the TD*X VTD process.

Scale-up data were collected during this commercial scale VTD demonstration test. Multiple
optimally sized full scale VTD units can be deployed for Hanford LAW treatment. The capacity
of those operations could be in excess of 7,000 gal-PTW/day if needed to support Hanford Tank
Waste Operations & Closure (H2C) processing requirements.

The demonstration test was conducted by TD*X at the EnergySolutions Treatment Storage and
Disposal Facility (TSDF) in Clive, Utah. TD*X performed this work for EnergySolutions as the
thermal desorption waste treatment subcontractor. EnergySolutions is the operator of the full
service TSDF and has used TD*X proprietary VTD system to meet organic LDR treatment
standards in support of their TSDF operation since 2002.

Based on the outstanding results of this demonstration test, with organic chemical and toxic metals
spiking to conservatively high levels in Hanford LAW simulant liquid, the many stakeholders of
the Hanford Tank Waste project can be confident the TD*X VTD treatment of PTW liquids from
the tanks will generate a residual waste form that is treated to both EPA and State standards for
safe disposal of hazardous and low-level radioactive waste. The aggressive treatment process itself
provides this assurance, as is preferred for this complicated waste matrix containing numerous
chemical and radioactive constituents. Furthermore, for over 20 years all features of this treatment
train have been commercially treating similar complicated mixed wastes from DOE sites including
Hanford. They are available to be accessed at the capacity scale required for timely completion of
the Hanford Tank Waste project.

 4

 SECTION 3 - SUMMARY

3.1 Objectives

A Demonstration Test Plan (TDX-25-001) dated February 21, 2025, was prepared by
EnergySolutions and TD*X to perform treatment with TD*X VTD system and evaluate the ability
of this technology to remove regulated organics from simulants representing the low activity waste
fraction of Hanford tank waste (Hanford LAW/PTW). Select organics and inorganics that are
reasonably expected to be present in Hanford LAW/PTW and associated with the waste codes
listed on the Hanford double-shell tank and single-shell tank RCRA Permit Part A1 (and associated
underlying hazardous constituents) were targeted for this demonstration. The list of reasonably
expected to be present organics was reviewed and organic spikes were selected to represent the
entire range of organic compound boiling points expected to be present in Hanford LAW/PTW.

Data and results from this work will be used to help support the development of a Sample-and-
Send approach to treating Hanford LAW. The Sample-and-Send strategy relies on sampling
retrieved waste for key attributes such as the concentration of Land Disposal Restrictions (LDR)
constituents and radionuclides used in 10 CFR 61.55 waste classification and transferring the waste
stream to pretreatment to remove solids and key radionuclides. When required to address LDR
organics treatment, the pretreated waste stream is then treated by an LDR organic removal or
destruction treatment technology such as the TD*X proprietary VTD system. The purpose of this
effort was to conduct additional demonstration tests to confirm that the selected regulated
compounds will be effectively, consistently, and routinely treated by removal through the TD*X
VTD process.

The demonstration test was conducted by TD*X at the EnergySolutions TSDF in Clive, Utah.
TD*X performed this work for EnergySolutions as the thermal desorption waste treatment partner.
EnergySolutions is the operator of the full service TSDF and has used TD*X proprietary VTD
system to meet organic LDR treatment standards in support of their TSDF operation since 2002.
EnergySolutions has a state-issued Part B permit (RCRA Permit) for the management and disposal
of RCRA hazardous mixed waste and has also secured permission from the Utah Division of Waste
Management and Radiation Control (DWMRC) for operation of the VTD unit under
EnergySolutions’ existing radioactive materials license. Attachment II-1-12 of the RCRA Permit
(a copy is provided in Appendix C of this plan) describes requirements of the VTD system. This
same VTD system is also designated as EPA and State of Utah approved for treatment of waste
assigned the treatment standard of combustion (CMBST) and is fully compliant with the CMBST

1
 LDR organic waste codes on the Hanford Double-Shell Tank Part A Permit include F001-F005
and a subset of D018-D043.
 11

requirements in 40 CFR 268.42 for those numerous problematic organic chemical constituents (see
40 CFR 268.44(o)).
The TD*X VTD consists of two principal parts: a thermal separation unit (dryer) and a gas
treatment unit. Contaminated solids are heated at relatively low temperatures in the thermal
separation unit, which is a totally enclosed dryer. The dryer is heated using an indirect heat source
that is fired by propane or natural gas. The dryer consists of a heated steel cylinder. The waste is
fed into the cylinder, where it is heated by conduction from the external source.

An inert gas (nitrogen) carries the volatilized materials from the dryer to the gas treatment system.
In the gas system, the carrier gas is cooled to condense and recover the organics and PCBs. The
treated carrier gas is returned to the dryer in a closed loop. A small amount of gas (consisting
primarily of non-condensable gases like nitrogen and oxygen) is continuously purged from the
system to maintain the desired oxygen concentration and control the vacuum in the dryer. The
purge stream (process vent) passes through a filter train that includes a final HEPA filter and a
carbon adsorption unit. This removes particulate material and further reduces the organic
contaminants prior to discharge to the atmosphere. Fresh nitrogen is introduced for makeup and
to control the oxygen concentration within the system. A simplified block flow diagram is
presented as Figure 5-6.

The H2C Chief Technology Office (CTO) requested this technical demonstration of the TD*X
VTD system to remove regulated organics from simulated Hanford LAW, allowing commercial
disposal of the system byproducts. Results of this demonstration validate that TD*X VTD system
is a robust and acceptable technology to treat Hanford LAW.

3.2 Scope of Work

This section provides an overview of the scope of work performed. Details are found in the
subsequent sections of this report.

Task 1: Prepare Simulant and Measure Concentrations of Organics and Inorganics
A representative simulant recipe and general preparation instructions were provided by H2C to
EnergySolutions. EnergySolutions/TD*X prepared a batch of the requested simulant as a base for
demonstration testing. The initial concentrations of the requested organics and regulated
inorganics were calculated based on actual weights spiked. Samples of the solidified spiked
simulant material were obtained for verification. Inorganic spikes were prepared by Pacific
Northwest National Laboratory (PNNL) and sent to EnergySolutions for inclusion in the test. The
final list and concentrations of organics and regulated inorganics to spike into the simulant were
developed by EnergySolutions in consultation with H2C based on lessons learned, information

 12

gained in the August 2024 testing, and the goal of identifying surrogate organics that cover the
volatility range of the 132 Land Disposal Restriction (LDR) organics that are reasonably expected
to be present in Hanford LAW/PTW.

Within this demonstration test, the spikes were all added to one of the four simulant drums
prepared for VTD treatment. Verification samples were taken from the solidified, spiked drum
immediately after the spikes were introduced and the simulant solidified.

Task 2: Solidify the Simulant and Perform a VTD Test
The simulant was solidified/stabilized using the typical ambient temperature process employed by
EnergySolutions/TD*X. Samples of the solidified/stabilized waste product were collected and
analyzed to determine the concentration of the spiked organics prior to VTD treatment. The
solidified/stabilized material was then treated using the VTD system.

For this demonstration test, the spikes were added concurrently with the simulant drum being
solidified.

Task 3: Measure the Concentrations of Organics and Inorganics After VTD Treatment
The concentrations of the spiked organic and inorganic compounds in the solidified/stabilized
material were measured after the VTD treatment and compared to the calculated concentration in
the initial liquid simulant and the pre-VTD-treated solid samples to evaluate the organics
removal/destruction performance. The final list of species to evaluate was developed by
EnergySolutions/TD*X in consultation with H2C based on lessons learned, information gained in
August 2024 testing, and the goal of identifying surrogate organics that cover the volatility range
of the 132 LDR reasonably expected to be present organics in Hanford LAW/PTW. The final list
of compounds and their applicable properties are listed in Tables 5-3 and 5-4 of the demonstration
test plan.

3.3 Previous Testing

This demonstration test was planned shortly after the submittal of the DT-1 test report on
October 31, 2024. Following review of the DT-1 test report by H2C it was apparent that a second
demonstration test would help further evaluate the capability of TD*X VTD to treat the 132
LDR reasonably expected to be present organics in Hanford LAW/PTW. Development of the
second demonstration test is further detailed in this report. A short summary of treatment results
for the spiked organics from DT-1 is shown below in Table 3-1. Calculated feed concentrations
are the concentration of each spike chemical in the four solidified feed drums that were used in
DT-1.

 higher levels to demonstrate a higher removal efficiency while also demonstrating the
 effectiveness of the TD*X VTD unit at removing a wider range of organic spikes.

 SECTION 4 - PROJECT ORGANIZATION

The project organization during the demonstration test was essentially the same as the organization
during routine operation of the VTD unit.

TD*X functions as part of an integrated project team, under contract with EnergySolutions.
EnergySolutions Operations Director has overall responsibility for site activities. The Operations
Director and staff manage the balance of activities in the restricted area, including waste receiving,
treatment residue disposal, management of the contamination reduction zone and support zone
facilities (all within the radiological restricted area). EnergySolutions Site Safety Officer serves
as a safety professional and project resource for implementation of the site’s health and safety plan.
All activities at the facility are performed under requirements of the site Radioactive Material
License, overseen by the site Radiation Safety Officer (RSO) and managed by field health physics
personnel.

TD*X is responsible for implementing waste treatment activities associated with the VTD unit.
TD*X Chief Lead Operator manages personnel and operations of the VTD. Reporting to the Chief
Lead are a Lead Operator and Plant Operator, who assist in day-to-day operations of the VTD unit.
Operational support is provided by the Process Engineer including planning, permitting, and
operational review. During the demonstration testing, the Process Engineer, directed by TD*X
Thermal Engineer, coordinated the activities of the operating crew and laboratory to assure that
the test requirements were met.

Supplemental testing and analytical laboratory services for the demonstration test was contracted
to GEL Laboratories LLC, by EnergySolutions. GEL is certified by the National Environmental
Laboratory Accreditation Program (NELAP) and performed analysis of solids (solidified simulant,
processed material), aqueous solutions (simulant), and oil (condensate) according to the sampling
and analysis plan. This lab works with the Clive site to implement most of the permit-required
process monitoring sample analyses.

All field personnel met the minimum safety training requirements of the OSHA 1910.120
Hazardous Waste Operations and Emergency Response (HAZWOPER) training standard.

 15

 SECTION 5 – TEST EXECUTION

5.1 Demonstration Test Summary

This section provides a summary of activities that were part of the demonstration test, including
simulant preparation and solidification, organic/inorganic spike addition, the demonstration test
run, and sampling.

5.1.1 Simulant Preparation and Solidification

TD*X successfully prepared approximately 120 gallons of liquid simulant for the demonstration
test on March 24-25, 2025. The simulant was prepared in accordance with the recipe and
preparation instructions provided by H2C. Four (4) 30-gallon simulant mixtures were prepared in
individual 55-gallon drums for subsequent solidification. The liquid simulant formulation is based
on empirical estimates of pre-treated LAW to be produced by the WATT Program and is a
nonradioactive representation of the liquid fraction of the Hanford Tank Waste materials. Target
and calculated simulant concentrations are listed in Table 5-1. Calculated simulant concentrations
were derived using the actual weight of each component added to the drums.

 Figure 5-1: Simulant Liquid in Sample Jar

The simulant drums were sealed and stored in a temperature-controlled environment awaiting the
addition of organic and inorganic spike chemicals. On March 26, 2025, one simulant drum was
solidified prior to the spike drum to test the equipment and validate mixing ability of the cement
mixer.

Organic chemical and toxic inorganic metals spiking of one of the prepared simulant drums was
performed on March 26, 2025. All the organic and inorganic spike compounds for this
demonstration test were introduced into this single drum in order to minimize handling and
evaporation losses (all four drums were fed into the VTD unit and mixed together in the
demonstration test run). A cement mixer was used to ensure adequate homogenization was
achieved when adding spike chemicals. For the spike drum (Drum 2) seven bags of diatomaceous
earth (DE) granular absorbent were placed into the mixer and the prepared simulant liquid was
pumped in as the cement mixer was operated. Inorganic spikes were added before organics to
minimize volatility loss of organics during solidification and spiking. Organic and inorganic spikes
were added into the mixer according to Table 5-2 below. Once inorganic chemicals had been
added, organic chemicals were spiked in the reverse order of their volatilities to minimize vapor
loss during spiking. All organic chemical spikes were in liquid form when added to the cement
mixer. Both m-Cresol and o-Cresol were stored, sealed, near an area heater on March 25, 2025, to
ensure they were above their melting points (52oF and 88oF respectively). While incorporating

 17

organic spikes, one and a half additional bags (37.5 lbs) of DE granular absorbent were added to
the mixer to ensure adequate solidification of the liquids.

Spike chemicals were selected from the list of 132 reasonably expected to be present organic
RCRA regulated chemicals associated with Hanford Tank Waste. The organic chemicals were
selected to bracket boiling point ranges. Figure 5-2 below shows the full range of boiling points
for the 132 organics reasonably expected to be present in Hanford PTW/LAW with the nine that
were selected for this demonstration test noted. The reason bis(2-ethylhexyl)phthalate was chosen
to represent the high range is that it is the highest boiling point organic that is a liquid without
having heat added. Using liquid organics was important for this demonstration test for spiking
purposes.

 Figure 5-2: Boiling Points of 132 Reasonably Expected to be Present Organics (oF)

The various ranges effect the targeted treatment temperature for thermal desorption. Some thermal
treatment technologies, such as simple evaporation, would be unable to treat the full range of
organics reasonably expected to be present in LAW. Note that many of the chemicals have boiling
points that are well above the boiling point of water and require targeting an aggressive operating
envelope to be able to achieve EPA’s LDR treatment standards. The LDR non-wastewater
concentration-based treatment standards are frequently quite low, at or near 1 part per million, as
well as near the method reporting limits for organic chemical testing.

After all spiking materials had been introduced, the mixer was operated for five minutes prior to
collecting representative samples. Sample IDs FEED-DT-2 and FEED-DT-2-DUP were collected
just prior to transferring the material into a drum. Figure 5-3 below shows the setup of the mixer,
drum, and pump used to solidify each drum.

 19

 Figure 5-3: Solidification Equipment

Once the spike materials were added and samples were collected, the prepared material was
transferred to Drum 2 as seen in Figure 5-4 below. The yellow tint of the solids appeared following
the addition of Na2CrO4∙4H2O and is believed to be staining from the chromium component of that
compound. Though additional DE absorbent was added, all liquids were not adequately absorbed.
This was not apparent until the material had been transferred back into the drum for storage and is
likely the main source of error between calculated and analytical spike concentrations in samples
FEED-DT-2 and FEED-DT-2-DUP. Once transferred back into the original drum, Drum 2 was
sealed and staged for VTD processing. Drum 2 was fed to the VTD in its entirety, ensuring that
all spike materials were processed as part of the batch. Due to the high concentration of nitrates in
 20

the simulant liquids, confidential solid reagents had to be added during the feed process. These
reagents were added at the same time as drum 2 to help absorb the free liquids shown in Figure 5-
4 during the feed process. Additionally, drum 2 was fed following the required DE absorbent (75
lbs), dry sand (906 lbs.), and drum 1 to ensure a dry bed of solids was present in the VTD dryer.

 Figure 5-4: Solidified Spike Drum. Note the presence of free liquid (brown puddle).

Once Drum 2 was sealed, the remaining two drums of simulant liquids were solidified using the
same procedure as described previously. None of the simulant drums contained visible solid
precipitates following transfer of the contents to the mixer, as seen in Figure 5-5 below, which
indicates all simulant components remained in solution.

 Figure 5-5: Empty Drum after Transfer of Simulant Liquid for Solidification

5.1.2 Demonstration Test

A single demonstration test run (batch) was performed starting on March 27, 2025, at 09:40 AM
and ending on March 28, 2025, at 05:55 AM. The VTD unit was operated at optimal processing
conditions as established during prior treatment operations for RCRA coded waste streams. The
test included sample collection of feed and treated solids (processed material in accordance with
Table 6-1 in the Demonstration Test Plan; as illustrated in Table 5-4, Sampling Plan Matrix,
herein).

The batch lasted a total of 20.25 hours and was discharged into a B-25 box at 5:55 AM on March
28, 2025. The batch duration was longer than typical VTD batch times of 8-12 hours but was more
than three and a half hours shorter than the batch duration for Demonstration Test 1 (DT-1). The
extended time was required due to the presence of nitrated compounds that were also present when
processing material from DT-1. The VTD unit has previously processed over 500 drums of Low-
Level Mixed Waste with high levels of both sodium nitrate and organic chemical content. No
adverse effects were observed as the simulant waste was heated through the decomposition range
of each nitrated compound, implying that batch duration could be reduced with additional
operating experience. It is expected that batch times would be in the normal range (8-12 hours) or
less after process optimization is performed.

 22

For the duration of the batch the VTD operated with stable unit operating parameters. Process
data measurements are presented in Table 5-3 below. These process monitoring data are reported
along with other supporting data described in Table 5-5, Summary of Monitoring Plan for VTD -
EnergySolutions Project. The process monitoring system was used throughout the duration of the
demonstration test to measure and record key operating parameters.

The demonstration test run duration was defined by the length of the treatment cycle,

Treated processed material sampling was performed after completion of the treatment cycle, when
the material was still hot in the product cooling hopper and prior to discharge into the B-25 box
on March 28, 2025. Samples were taken from the product cooling hopper rather than the storage
container to minimize the potential of cross contamination from process transfer equipment or the
storage container.

 25

The total weight of material introduced to the dryer, including reagents, clean sand, and dry
absorbent was 3,549 pounds. Due to the level of nitrates present in the simulant liquid, the VTD
Nitrate Processing Plan was followed. An additional 1,551 lbs. of DE absorbent, dry sand, and
confidential reagents are added into the VTD dryer with the feed waste to help lower the total
nitrate concentration and control the nitrate decomposition reaction during processing. The product
and condensate weights were 2,460 and 1,274 pounds respectively, or 3,734 pounds total coming
out of the system. Based on these weights 105.2% of material fed to the plant was recovered during
the demonstration test run. This falls within the 90-110% recovery range that is typically expected
for an individual VTD treatment batch and confirms that the treatment process is separation, not
destruction. Weights fluctuate up and down due to the release of previously deposited material in
parts of the dryer, product discharge system, and condensate tanks between batches.
Approximately 185 pounds of material from previous operations and initial charging of the
condensate tanks was present in this batch, leading to slightly more than 100% of the mass being
recovered. This phenomenon is consistent with historical TD*X VTD operations and occurs due
to tank levels being netted out using a sight glass and the tendency of fine solids to stick to surfaces
in the product discharge system. Figure 5-7 below shows treated solids in the product cooling
hopper following the discharge of the batch.

 Figure 5-7: Treated Solids in Product Cooling Hopper

5.2 Sampling and Analysis Activities

During the demonstration test, the processed material was sampled and system operating
parameters monitored to demonstrate compliance with the specified performance criteria.
Sampling was performed to ensure compliance with the RCRA LDR Universal Treatment
Standards (UTS, see Title 40, Code of Federal Regulations, Part 268.48). Duplicate samples and
equipment blank samples were also collected from the solids sampling locations for quality
control. This section provides details regarding the sampling and monitoring locations that were
used during the demonstration test and provides a summary of each of the sampling and laboratory
analysis procedures. Codified methods were used, primarily from the US EPA's SW-846 test
methods for solid waste. In all cases, the most current method was used.

The sampling locations on the VTD unit are designed to capture desired process streams of bulk
solids. Spiked solids samples were collected from the mixing apparatus just prior to being
transferred and sealed in a labeled storage drum. Processed solids samples were collected from the
product cooling system just after being discharged from the VTD dryer and prior to being
conveyed into a B-25 box. US EPA methods and apparatus were used to collect and handle these
samples, as presented in Section 8. Table 5-4 presents the Sampling Plan Matrix for this
demonstration test.

5.2.1 Spike and Waste Feed Sample

As described in Section 4.1.1, simulant liquids were solidified with granular absorbent DE material
before being spiked with organic and inorganic material (Figure 5-8). Once spiking was complete,
the resulting material was allowed to mix for five minutes and then the FEED-DT-2 and
FEED-DT-2-DUP samples were collected from the cement mixer just prior to the material being
transferred into a drum and sealed. Multiple aliquots were collected during operation of the cement
mixer using a stainless-steel spoon. Samples were placed into a stainless-steel bowl, as seen in
Figure 5-9 below, and mixed thoroughly with the stainless-steel spoon. This mixture was
transferred into the appropriate sample containers described in Table 5-4.
 Figure 5-8: Spike Additions

 Inorganic spike addition (Ni(NO3)2∙6H2O) Organic spike addition (m-Cresol)

 Figure 5-9: Feed Sample Collection

 30

5.2.2 Treated Product Sample

The treated processed material sample was taken directly from the product cooling hopper
immediately after the treatment run was completed and the waste dropped into the hopper. This
“product” is the residual desorber solids from the VTD process. The sampling technician stood on
a small step stool to collect this sample from the product cooling hopper as seen in Figure 5-10.

 Figure 5-10: Sampling from the Product Cooling Hopper

The product sample was taken with a stainless-steel spoon directly from a port in the product
cooling hopper, placed in a stainless-steel bowl, and covered with foil. The sample was tightly
covered and allowed to cool to room temperature prior to placement in the sample containers, and
labeled as PROC-DT-2 and duplicate sample PROC-DT-2-DUP.

An additional 3-liters of treated product sample were jarred and labelled PROC-DT-2-PNNL to
support further treatment by PNNL (stabilization of metals and TCLP testing). The sample jars
were filled from the sampling bowl that was used for PROC-DT-2 and PROC-DT-2-DUP. Labeled
samples were shipped to PNNL on April 1, 2025.

 31

5.2.3 Duplicate Samples and Equipment Blank

During the demonstration test it was necessary to collect both duplicate samples and equipment
blanks for quality control purposes. The number, location, and frequency of these quality
assurance samples is described in Table 5-4, Sampling Plan Matrix. Duplicates and equipment
blanks were named with an appropriate suffix relative to the sampling location that they assess; -
DUP for duplicate and -EB for equipment blank.

Duplicate solid samples of both the feed (FEED-DT-2-DUP) and processed material (PROC-DT-
2-DUP) were taken as separate containers from the mixed composite sample in the stainless-steel
bowls. The processed material sampling equipment blank (PROC-DT-2-EB) was collected from a
deionized water rinse of the sampling equipment (stainless-steel spoon and mixing bowl) prior to
collecting the processed material samples.

5.2.4 Solids Sampling Method Waste Feed and Treated Product Solids

The SW-846 sampling method of grab sampling with a scoop was used for collecting waste feed
and treated product solids samples. The sampling technician wore clean disposable nitrile gloves
when handling the sampling equipment and sample containers. A clean stainless-steel spoon was
used for each sample. The samples were collected from the waste feed mixer and product cooling
hopper, respectively, and placed into a clean covered stainless-steel bowl. At the end of the
sampling interval, the sample was thoroughly mixed (with a clean stainless steel spoon), and the
sample containers filled, labeled, and logged into the operations logbook and chain of custody
document.

The sampling equipment, the spoon and bowl, were decontaminated prior to each use. This
decontamination involved washing with a non-phosphate laboratory detergent, Alconox, rinsing
with clean tap water, and then final rinsing with reagent grade distilled water.

5.2.5 Sample Containers, Labels, Chain of Custody

Sample labeling is described in Table 5-4 and in Sections 4.2.1 through 4.2.4 above. The
management of samples is described in the Clive site procedure CL-LB-PR-003, Sample Control.
A copy of this procedure is provided in Appendix D.

 32

Sample containers and preservation were used as required per the appropriate test method
(Table 5-4). Sample containers were labeled, and their location and unit operating conditions at
the time of sampling were logged into the operations logbook. Sample container identifying
information was recorded on a chain-of-custody (COC) document used by the project laboratory
and communicated to the lab. A representative copy of the COC document is included in the
Sample Control procedure. All personnel performing sampling have received training in the
EnergySolutions Sample Control procedure prior to performing the sampling activities.
EnergySolutions laboratory staff performed the sample shipping for all samples.

Samples were shipped to the laboratory (GEL) in sample coolers with sufficient “blue ice” to
ensure their arrival at the preservation temperature of 4 ºC (as needed). The COC was included in
the sample cooler. Packing material was provided to keep glass containers from breaking during
shipment.

 Figure 5-11: Sealed PROC-DT-2 Sample

5.2.6 Laboratory Analysis Procedures

In general, codified test methods were used for demonstration test activities. Full level IV reporting
has been provided with summary results, case narrative, chromatograms, LCS/LCSD results, and
instrument calibration reports. The specific test methods utilized are summarized in Table 5-4.

 33

5.2.7 Health and Safety Issues While Sampling

All sampling activities were performed inside the exclusion zone (radioactive restricted area) with
the exception of the FEED-DT-2 and FEED-DT-2-DUP samples that were collected outside of the
exclusion zone, where the material was prepared. Exposure to site contaminants is potentially
possible in this area. All personnel were required to wear proper radioactive dosimetry and be
logged into the area under a Radiation Work Permit (RWP) for sampling inside the exclusion zone.

Personal protective equipment (PPE) required for feed sample collection was determined
following review of the spike materials SDS. For the collection of processed material (PROC
sampling) inside the exclusion zone, the personal protective equipment (PPE) was specified in the
activity-specific RWP. PPE for both sampling activities was level C, with Tyvek coverall, nitrile
gloves, and air purifying respirator. The inclusion of rubber boots was required for the processed
material sample that was taken in the exclusion zone.

5.3 Process Monitoring

The full, normal continuous process monitoring system (CPMS) for the VTD unit functioned
during the duration of the demonstration test. This section provides a summary discussion of those
key process monitoring parameters. Table 5-5 is included here as a summary of the monitoring
plan parameters. Key process monitoring data is summarized. Table II-1-12-A of the
EnergySolutions Part B Permit (included as Appendix C of the Demonstration Test Plan) provides
a list of all monitored VTD system parameters. The complete data for the demonstration test run
is retained by TD*X as proprietary process data and will be available to support technical
discussions as necessary. All process monitoring is subject to quality control as described in
Section 4.4.

5.3.1 Feed Mass

The feed preparation area has a platform scale. The feed scale was used to determine the mass of
waste fed during the demonstration test run. This was done by recording the full and empty weight
of each drum fed to the unit and logging this information. The mass of waste fed during the run
was determined by the difference (Table 5-3).

5.3.2 Feed Rate

The feed mass that was included in Table 5-3 was used along with the batch duration to calculate
the feed rate of the batch. The mass of waste fed during the run was divided by the batch duration,
in hours, to provide a lb/hr feed rate for the batch.

5.3.3 Treated Product Solids Temperature

The treated solids temperature was monitored using a Type K thermocouple in the dryer. This
thermocouple was connected to the programmable logic controller (PLC) data management
system, and its readings were recorded electronically and displayed on the computer graphic at the
operator console. This temperature measurement location provided a solids temperature at the exit
of the process and was used as a guide to judge performance of the unit. The operator adjusted the
treatment cycle time to maintain the treated product solids temperature above a threshold
determined by historical operations, which resulted in treated solids with an acceptably low
residual organic contamination level (below UTS).

5.4 – QUALITY ASSURANCE (QA) Activities

The standard QA plan for operation of the unit under the TSCA permit was followed. The
EnergySolutions corporate QA program was provided in Appendix D of the Demonstration Test
Plan. It provides upper-level quality requirements of all testing events performed on
EnergySolutions property. Other on-site QA requirements are incorporated into operating
procedures.

In general, QA for all samples and analyses required of this demonstration test were based on the
US EPA SW-846 methods implemented. These methods are noted in Table 5-4. Table 5-4 and
Section 4.2.4 also describe additional QA sampling incorporated as part of this test.

Duplicate samples were analyzed for FEED-DT-2 and PROC-DT-2 to evaluate for potential
sampling or analysis error. An equipment blank (EB) sample was analyzed for PROC-DT-2 to
evaluate potential contamination from sampling equipment.
 36

 SECTION 6 – TEST RESULTS

The primary objectives of the sampling and monitoring plan during the demonstration
were:
 • Demonstrate the ability of TD*X VTD technology to remove regulated organics from
 Hanford LAW/PTW such that that the treated solids are RCRA LDR compliant and meet
 UTS levels for the target organic constituents (Table 6-3);
 • Monitor process operating parameters to verify compliance with existing limits established
 in the EnergySolutions Clive Facility state-issued Part B Permit and to establish appropriate
 operating conditions for future campaigns.

6.1 Process Operating Parameters

During the demonstration test run the VTD process was operated at normal conditions for RCRA
coded mixed waste treatment. The following list presents actual ranges for these parameters during
the demonstration test run:

Size of Batch. Four (4) 55-gallon drums of solidified simulant (one containing measured organic
and inorganic spike contaminants) were fed to the VTD unit for the demonstration test. All four
drums were fed separately, in a short duration, to begin the batch. The total weight of solidified
simulant fed to the plant was 1,998 lb.

Dryer Draft Pressure (vacuum). Dryer internal pressure was maintained at an average of
565 mmHg during normal operations. The minimum vacuum observed was 533 mmHg and the
maximum vacuum observed was 746 mmHg. Pressure above ambient (645 mmHg) were only
temporary spikes that occur during normal operation of the VTD when filters are cleared. Periods
of positive pressure lasted no longer than one minute. This is not a key parameter for separation.
Rather, the internal pressure provides draft to draw vapors out of the dryer, and control fugitive
emissions.

Holding Time at Temperature. The required heating for the batch was 77% of the VTD unit’s
routinely achievable solids temperature and holding for a minimum of 12 minutes. The product
solids were held for 38 minutes above the treatment temperature prior to determining that the batch
cycle was complete. The specific temperature range used for treatment is considered proprietary
and not discussed in this report.

Minimum Process Temperature, Product Solids. Due to the nature of some spike chemicals
requiring CMBST coded treatment technology, the VTD minimum product solids temperature was
greater than 77% of the VTD unit’s routinely achievable solids temperature. The maximum solids
temperature observed during the batch was 79.3%.
 37

6.2 Sampling Results

The following discussion presents the results for the feed and product samples, along with the
associated field quality control samples along with a summary of results from the PNNL EPA
Method 1311 analysis of the VTD product. Details of the PNNL analysis are documented in a
separate report (WFDS-GD-001).

6.2.1 Pretreatment Results

Table 6-1 below provides a summary of the data for spiked organics analyzed within the
FEED-DT-2 and FEED-DT-2-DUP samples and provides their calculated spike concentrations
based on weight measurements. Calculations and sample results are for Drum 2, which was the
only drum spiked with organic and inorganic materials. Due to the variability of readily available
quantities for purchase, and a desire to consume all chemicals obtained, entire volumes of
procured spikes were used when spiking drum 2. This results in a high variability in the actual
overshoot of spiking between each of the organic spikes.

 39

Spike analytical results for organics suggest there was error in sampling or during laboratory
analysis. This is shown in Table 6-1 above by comparing the listed Solid Spike Drum
Concentration with the analytical results listed as FEED-DT-2 measured and FEED-DT-2-DUP
measured. The most likely explanation for error between the calculated and analytical results is
sampling error due to non-homogeneity of the solids and the presence of a small amount of free
liquids following solidification, as seen in Figure 5-4. These free liquids were not apparent while
the mixer was running due to the material actively mixing and holding these liquids on the outside
of the granules. The liquids only became apparent after the cement mixer had been stopped and
the material was transferred into the drum for storage. Since samples had already been collected
directly from the mixer, it was determined that analyzing the existing samples was the best course
of action due to volatilities of the organic spikes and potential loss associated with transferring
material back into the mixer for further solidification. The use of a mixer was planned to help
reduce the risk of inhomogeneity in the spiked drum sample but may have introduced additional
error due to the inability to determine adequate solidification prior to sampling.

Analytical results were sought only to confirm that spike chemicals were present in the feed and
not to be used for removal efficiency calculations. Measured weights and their associated
calculated spike concentrations are used for these calculations because the spike weight
measurements eliminate the error introduced by non-homogeneity and sampling error.

The only non-detectable organic spike was ethyl acetate, which was spiked well above lab
detection limits. One possible reason ethyl acetate was not detected is lack of absorption into the
DE granular absorbent as it became more saturated. Ethyl acetate was the third to last spike added.
As a result of the mixer being emptied as it was transferred to the drum, and the fact that two
additional drums were solidified following the spike drum using the same mixer, it is reasonable
to assume that the spiked amount made it into the batch as a free liquid that was absorbed into
other materials fed to the VTD unit.

Overall, organic spike analytical results are low. As suggested in the Test Plan, measured weight
values for spike chemicals will be used to determine viability of VTD for the removal of organics
from LAW tank waste. Non-homogeneity of spiked solid samples, including incomplete
absorption into the DE absorbent, is the most likely cause of the error between calculated and
analytical results. Low sample volumes relative to the batch size of 30-gallons make this the most
likely source of variability. Calculated liquid spike amounts (column 3 of Table 6-1) met the target
goal of at least twenty (20) times the non-wastewater (NWW) LDR limits. The average analytical
concentrations of FEED-DT-2 and FEED-DT-2-DUP are shown in Table 6-2 below along with
their corresponding NWW LDR limits. Minimum spiking targets were confirmed to be exceeded
for acetonitrile, 2-butanone, o-cresol, and m-cresol. Diethyl phthalate and bis(2-ethylhexyl)

 40

  phthalate were close to the twenty (20) times target with both exceeding seventeen (17) times the
  NWW LDR standard. Ethyl ether was well below twenty (20) times the NWW LDR standard but
  still was verified to be spiked at greater than seven (7) times the NWW LDR standard. Dimethyl
  phthalate analytical results were lower than NWW LDR standard for spiking while ethyl acetate
  was not detected. It is suspected that there was interference with detecting both ethyl acetate and
  dimethyl phthalate, as the calculated spike levels for each were more than enough to exceed twenty
  (20) times the NWW LDR standard.

6.2.3 PNNL Solidification Samples

As part of DT-2 samples PROC-DT-2-PNNL were collected and shipped to PNNL to perform a
stabilization study on inorganic spikes. Due to inorganic contaminants expected to be present in
Hanford tank LAW, once solidified LAW has been processed through the TD*X VTD, it may
need stabilization to meet UTS LDR treatment standards for inorganic contaminants. The
inorganic spikes are reasonably expected to be present in LAW. Spike levels were determined
based on three times the maximum levels expected in the 200-W area tank waste. These amounts
were spiked to simulate those levels in the simulant liquids prior to solidification for VTD, which
replicates the expected process. Spike concentrations and Toxicity Characteristic Leaching
Procedure (TCLP) results are listed below in Table 6-4.

Upon receipt by PNNL, two separate studies were performed with PROC-DT-2-PNNL. For one
study, the solids were stabilized according to the recipe provided by EnergySolutions to generate
product-treated solids (VTD-PT). For the other study, the VTD-PT solids, that were treated to
stabilize RCRA LDR regulated inorganic species according to the EnergySolutions recipe, were
grouted and solidified (VTD-GS) with the recommended addition of Portland lime cement (PLC).
Both the VTD-PT and VTD-GS samples were cured for 28 days prior to leach testing. After the
28-day cure time, the untreated VTD solids (control sample), cured VTD-PT, and cured VTD-GS
were tested according to EPA Method 1311. The two treated samples, VTD-PT and VTD-GS, both
produced TCLP leachates that met the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency’s universal
treatment standards (UTS) for non-wastewaters for all the LDR inorganics associated with
Hanford tank waste that were included in the test (note that Be was excluded from testing since
handling this material represents an extreme health hazard and Be is known to be below the NWW
 42

standard in tank waste). The untreated VTD solids met the UTS limits for all LDR inorganics
except for Cr, which exceeded the UTS limit, showing that inorganics treatment of the VTD solids
is required for disposal. This study and results are described in detail in WFDS-GD-001:Vacuum
Thermal Desorption Product Waste Forms Processing and Performance, which is included as
Appendix E of this report.

6.2.4 Quality Control Samples

Quality control sampling included duplicates of the feed and processed material samples as well
as an equipment blank of the processed material sampling equipment. Duplicate sample results
are noted in Table 6-2 and relative percent differences between the primary and duplicate samples
ranged from 30% to 73% for detected organics feed samples FEED-DT-2 and FEED-DT-2-DUP.
This difference can be attributed to a combination of sampling error (from sample heterogeneity)
and analytical error (method sensitivity and reproducibility). The presence of a small amount of
free liquids following spike simulant solidification may also be an indicator of feed sample
heterogeneity, but it is unknown to what degree. Processed material samples PROC-DT-2 and
PROC-DT-2-DUP showed excellent reproducibility with the only differences being the result in
MDL values between the two samples and all results being below those detection limits.

The equipment blank sample (PROC-DT-2-EB) showed multiple results for spike chemicals that
did not affect the results for PROC-DT-2 or PROC-DT-2-DUP. Results for Ethyl Ether, 2-
 43

Butanone, and Bis(2-ethylhexyl)Phthalate all came back slightly above method detection limits
(MDL) but did not show up in the processed solids analytical results above MDL. Detection limits
for the liquid equipment blank sample were much lower than they were for the processed solids
sample. Each of the three results are well below what would have been detected in the solids and
the result for Ethyl Ether is below the reporting limit (RL) and is shown as an estimation between
the MDL and RL. Due to the difference in the detection limits, these should not be considered as
an impact to the results of the demonstration test. 2-Butanone was detected at a concentration that
exceeded 5% of the sample result. This could be indicative of equipment or laboratory
contamination that would imply sample results could be biased high. However, since the samples
are reported at the detection limit, any kind of bias correction would be inappropriate.

 44

 SECTION 7 – WASTE HANDLING AND DISPOSAL

Treated waste from the demonstration testing activities met the RCRA UTS requirements for both
the organic and inorganic constituents associated with Hanford tank waste. The waste will require
stabilization treatment for inorganics (chromium) prior to disposal in the EnergySolutions Mixed
Waste Landfill cell. The treated material will be permanently disposed in compliance with LDR.

Aqueous and organic condensates were accumulated in the process tanks on the VTD. The
condensate was then transferred to 330-gallon tote containers. The organic and aqueous
condensate has essentially no radioactivity, well below levels that are typically present in
background fuel oil. The condensate is analyzed by radioactive assay to confirm this and then
shipped to a separate radioactive material licensed facility that is operated by TD*X for free release
and authorized disposal at a permitted commercial waste disposal facility.

 45

 • The VTD technology can remove regulated organics from Hanford LAW such that that the

 constituents.

efficiency to a lower value. For example, Ethyl Ether, which was spiked more than three times
any other compound, was removed at greater than 99.999%. The treatment process removal
efficiency has been used for reviewing LDR treatment variance applications in the past. The above
measured treatment process removal efficiencies are all greater than those used to characterize
highly effective treatment and risk reduction.

 SECTION 9 – PROCESS DATA ANALYSIS

9.1 Process Operating Conditions

Thorough review of process data from the demonstration test demonstrates fully that the LAW
tank waste is a good candidate for TD*X VTD processing. Treatment temperatures were easily
achieved and there were no upset conditions throughout the duration of the batch.

Batch duration was longer than a typical batch due to the cautious approach to processing nitrate
impacted materials through the VTD unit. Though batch duration was still longer than a typical
VTD batch, overall treatment time improved by more than three hours on this, the second batch,
processing this material. Care is taken to ensure that nitrate decomposition does not occur rapidly
due to the oxidizing nature of the decomposition reaction. With multiple nitrated compounds being
present in the simulant liquids, this required heating rates to be reduced for each of them. There
were no signs of nitrate decomposition reactions proceeding at a rate that the VTD could not safely
manage. This indicates that heating rates could be increased through each of the nitrate
decomposition ranges to reduce batch time.

Pressure spikes up to maximum of 746 mmHg did occur, but only momentarily while nitrogen was
introduced to clear filters of particulate matter. Vacuum immediately returned to the process as
expected and as is normal for VTD operations and does not affect the efficiency of the treatment
process or adversely affects human health or the environment.

9.2 Automatic Waste Feed Cut-off (AWFCO) System

The VTD is equipped with an AWFCO system that is active any time that contaminated material
is being fed to the unit. The AWFCO parameters are presented in Table 9-1 and are established in
the TD*X TSCA approvals. The AWFCO system function is demonstrated as a weekly operating
procedure.

 49

 SECTION 10 – QUALITY ASSURANCE RESULTS

The analytical laboratory (GEL) made an assessment of the Quality Assurance results before
submitting individual sample results. The QA/QC data are presented in the individual laboratory
reports. The data were found to be valid and within acceptable QA/QC limits.

Specific samples were taken during the testing program to assess the quality of both the sampling
and analysis procedures. The results of these samples are summarized in Tables 6-2, 6-3, and 6-
4.

The results of the primary and duplicate samples for the feed showed relative percent differences
ranging from 30% to 73% for detected organics, possibly due to a combination of sampling error
and analytical error, or sample heterogeneity, but the feed samples confirmed the presence of
organics as intended. The treated processed materials both showed excellent (100%) repeatability,
as all results were less than detectable. Also, the equipment blank sample results had very low
concentrations for the volatile and semi-volatile spike contaminants analyzed. Only two were
detected above reporting limits with one detected above the method detection limit but below the
reporting limit.

Equipment blank samples taken from the processed solids sampling equipment indicate that
possible minor sample contamination may have occurred during sampling activities for Ethyl
Ether, 2-Butanone, and bis(2-ethylhexyl) phthalate. Though some contamination may have
occurred, treatment of the solids was adequate to still result in all PROC-DT-2 and PROC-DT-2-
DUP results below method detection limits.

There were high amounts of variability between analytical and calculated results for the VTD feed.
Potential sampling error, handling losses, and heterogeneity of feed samples demonstrate why
calculated concentrations should be used to determine test success criteria. Variability was
expected during test planning and lab analysis was performed as a form of quality control, not as
the primary source for evaluating test success criteria.

 51

 SECTION 11 – CONCLUSIONS AND RECOMMENDATIONS

11.1 VTD Compliance with RCRA LDR

The non-wastewater treatment standards from EPA’s LDR program in 40 CFR 268 were achieved
by TD*X VTD treatment of the Hanford LAW simulant material. This was the expected result
because the TD*X VTD process is robust and has consistently and frequently achieved this result
for over 21 years treating LLMW at the Clive facility from most of the DOE Complex
facilities. This is especially important because the LAW fraction of Hanford LAW is expected to
contain LDR organic constituents with high boiling points that are difficult for most technologies
to treat to LDR standards. TD*X VTD demonstrates the ability to remove these difficult LDR
organics at processing rates and concentrations far above other commercial VTD technologies.
Furthermore, Hanford LAW also carries the F001-F005 spent solvent waste codes for certain
organics that have been listed by EPA as well as a subset of EPA’s D018-D043 waste codes for
organic chemicals. EnergySolutions and TD*X have demonstrated that the Clive VTD unit has
successfully removed the LDR organics from the spiked simulant. TD*X VTD has been shown to
be an effective treatment technology for Hanford LAW and an appropriate selection for treatment
of LDR organic contaminants.

11.2 VTD Scale-Up to Hanford Capacity Requirement

The demonstration test yielded technical data that are required to prepare a preliminary production
scale-up projection for treatment of LAW materials at the Hanford site. The projected batch time
for a VTD unit specifically designed for processing LAW/PTW material is 6 to 8 hours. This unit
would be engineered with a processing capacity of more than 140 cu.ft. of solidified LAW/PTW
per batch. The resulting LAW processing rate would be greater than 2,400 gal/day per
unit. Multiple units of this size would be deployed to meet the required daily LAW material
processing capacity.

11.3 VTD Applicability to Sample and Send Approach

Through two demonstration tests, the TD*X VTD process has proven high level treatment of
simulated Hanford tank LAW. The two demonstration tests have validated the removal of fifteen
(15) of the 132 organic compounds that are reasonably expected to be present in Hanford
LAW/PTW. The fifteen (15) compounds selected represent the full range of volatilities that can
be reasonably expected in Hanford LAW, demonstrating that TD*X VTD is appropriate for
treating this waste under a sample and send approach.

 52

 Calibration Information

 Continuing Calibration Verification Requirements
 All Calibration Verification Standards (CCV) did not meet the acceptance criteria as outlined in Method 8260D for
 samples and the associated QC. However, the method allows for a designated number of outliers dependent on the
 requested analyte list. This SDG satisfied the 8260D outlier acceptance criteria. The results are reported.

 Laboratory Control Sample (LCS) Recovery
 The LCS and/or LCSD (See Below) recoveries were not within the acceptance limits for all analytes. The
 unacceptable analytes were not detected in the samples associated with the laboratory control samples. Therefore, the
 data were reported.

 Holding Time Specifications
 GEL assigns holding times based on the associated methodology, which assigns the date and time from sample
 collection or sample receipt. Those holding times expressed in hours are calculated in the ALPHALIMS system.
 Those holding times expressed as days expire at midnight on the day of expiration. Samples (See Below) were not
 analyzed within holding because of a required dilution that occurred outside the holding time. The results are
 qualified accordingly.

 Some target compounds were not included in the LCS/LCSD and/or MS/MSD in this batch. A representative list of
 compounds were spiked and monitored. The lab is in the process of acquiring additional standards in order to fortify
 the LCS/LCSD and/or MS/MSD with all requested target compounds. 716555001 (HT 2 Feed-DT-2) and 716555002
         (HT 2 Feed-DT-2-DUP).

 Samples 716555001 (HT 2 Feed-DT-2) and 716555002 (HT 2 Feed-DT-2-DUP) did not meet the client requested
 permit limits for multiple target analytes. The samples were listed as RAD 1 Hazard Code and were extracted using
 10 grams (not the method prescribed 30 grams). The samples were analyzed at a dilution due to the presence of
 non-target analytes and due to the presence of over range target analytes. The data were reported.
 Diphenylamine Statement
 Diphenylamine has superseded the reporting of N-Nitroso-diphenylamine. As per the EPA, N-Nitroso-diphenylamine
 decomposes in the gas chromatographic inlet and cannot be separated from Diphenylamine. Studies of these two
 compounds at GEL, both independent of each other and together, showed that they not only co-elute, but also have
 similar mass spectra. N-Nitroso-diphenylamine and Diphenylamine are therefore reported as Diphenylamine on all
 reports and forms.

 1206058267 Laboratory Control Sample (LCS)

 The samples in this SDG were analyzed on an "as received" basis.

 Diphenylamine Statement
 Diphenylamine has superseded the reporting of N-Nitroso-diphenylamine. As per the EPA, N-Nitroso-diphenylamine
 decomposes in the gas chromatographic inlet and cannot be separated from Diphenylamine. Studies of these two
 compounds at GEL, both independent of each other and together, showed that they not only co-elute, but also have
 similar mass spectra. N-Nitroso-diphenylamine and Diphenylamine are therefore reported as Diphenylamine on all
 reports and forms.

 Sample Analyte Value
         1206058730MS and 1206058731MSD (Non SDG 717039001) Pyridine RPD 93* (0%-30%)

 Diphenylamine Statement
 Diphenylamine has superseded the reporting of N-Nitroso-diphenylamine. As per the EPA, N-Nitroso-diphenylamine
 decomposes in the gas chromatographic inlet and cannot be separated from Diphenylamine. Studies of these two
 compounds at GEL, both independent of each other and together, showed that they not only co-elute, but also have
 similar mass spectra. N-Nitroso-diphenylamine and Diphenylamine are therefore reported as Diphenylamine on all
 reports and forms.

Sample Control Revision 2

1 PURPOSE AND SCOPE
 1.1 Purpose
 To establish sampling requirements for incoming waste shipments and provide a
 comprehensive plan for the management and disposal of samples collected from
 waste shipments and ongoing site operations as overseen by the on-site Chemistry
 Laboratory. Also, to define the Chain of Custody documentation requirements for
 samples taken and acceptance criteria for the management of pre-shipment and
 treatability study waste samples on the EnergySolutions, Clive Facility.
 1.2 Scope
 This procedure applies to all of the Chemistry Laboratory area and personnel, and
 all samples received or collected in conjunction with waste acceptance and
 processing, as well as on-site process sampling.

2 REFERENCES
 2.1 CL-LB-PR-004, Incoming Chemical Screening Parameters
 2.2 CL-LB-PR-005, Laboratory Instrument Calibration & Maintenance
 2.3 CL-LB-PR-041, Analytical Data Review
 2.4 CL-SH-PR-150, Laboratory Chemical Hygiene Plan
 2.5 CL-IN-PR-450, Instrument Operation
 2.6 Radioactive Material License - UT 2300249
 • Waste Characterization Plan
 2.7 State-issued Part B Permit
 • Attachment II-1, Waste Analysis Plan
 • Attachment II-1-4, Liquid Waste Management Plan
 • Attachment II-1-10, Management of Wastes Containing Polychlorinated
 Biphenyls (PCBs)
 • Attachment II-1-10.1, Management of Wastes Containing Polychlorinated
 Biphenyls (PCBs) at the LLRW Facility
 2.8 CL-MD-PR-300, Onsite Waste Management
 2.9 11e.(2) Radioactive Material License - UT 2300478
 2.10 CL-SR-PR-041, Incoming Radioactive Waste Shipment Acceptance

 2.12 CL-CH-PR-252, Incoming Shipment Sampling and Inspection
