Non-Proprietary Page 3 of 27

 2.14 CL-TN-PR-100, Clive Training Procedure
 2.15 CL-QA-PR-005, Quality Assurance Records

3 GENERAL
 3.1 Definitions
 3.1.1 Abandoned Sample - a sample that has been left without proper custody
 documentation and/or in an unsecured location or area. All abandoned
 samples shall be disposed of properly and requested to be re-sampled.
 3.1.2 Acutely Hazardous Waste – waste that contains highly toxic compounds
                     listed in 40 CFR 261.33(e).
 3.1.3 Approved for Management – authorization provided by trained Chemistry
 Laboratory personnel to store, treat or dispose of the waste material from a
 shipment, once sampling requirements have been fulfilled.
 3.1.4 BI Publisher – a computer based reporting program that takes incoming
 shipment and sampling information from the Electronic Tracking System
 and compiles it into practical reports for shipment management purposes.
 3.1.5 Chain of Custody (COC) – a record of the sequences of individuals who
 had custody of a sample or a group of samples.
 3.1.6 Composite Sample – a sample made up of aliquots from various containers
 and/or locations.
 3.1.7 Contact Waste – any material (i.e. Personal Protective Equipment (PPE),
 chemical analysis materials and residues, etc.) that come in contact with
 hazardous waste (including Mixed Waste).
 3.1.8 Custody Holder – individual having custody of a sample or group of
 samples.
 Note: Only Clive Chemistry Laboratory personnel can hold
 custody of a sample as a group. All other Sample
 Custody shall be held by an individual person.
 3.1.9 Custody Seal – a physical barrier used as a means to determine if access to
 the sample (and thus possible tampering) occurred between the time the
 sample was collected and the time the seal is broken upon receipt at the
 laboratory. Seals, when affixed properly, may be used in lieu of
 continuous physical possession. Seals shall be attached, in such a way that
 the sample container, collection bag, or shipping container cannot be
 opened without breaking the seal.
 3.1.10 Deferred Chemical (DC) – analysis for chemical constituents, usually
 performed by a Third Party Laboratory.
 3.1.11 Discrete Sample – a single sample taken from an individual container
 and/or location.

Non-Proprietary Page 4 of 27

Sample Control Revision 2

 3.1.12 Electronic Chain of Custody Database – a database designed to track on-
 site and off-site sample transfers by use of an electronic COC document.
 3.1.13 Electronic Document Imaging System (OnBase) - A client / server based
 PC application for storing and retrieving a wide range of information.
 3.1.14 Electronic Tracking System– a centralized relational database management
 systems application that provides information on EnergySolutions’
 customers, their waste streams, and the waste material sent to
 EnergySolutions for management and disposal. The information
 contained in the database is obtained from required hard copy quality
 assurance documentation, electronic imports, and real-time entry.
 3.1.15 Extraordinary Sample- any out of the ordinary sample that may require
 chain-of-custody tracking and/or documentation (i.e. waste spill clean-up,
 re-sampling, etc.).
 3.1.16 Generator Packet Information – generator and waste stream specific
 documents, available on On-Base database, detailing the waste stream
 information (to include presence of hazardous material, low-level
 radioactive material, special nuclear material, PCB material, or asbestos
 material) and waste physical properties & package information (to include
 general characteristics, material size, moisture content, waste shipping &
 packaging and narrative description and history of waste).
 3.1.17 Hazardous Waste – waste that demonstrates a characteristic of ignitability,
 corrosivity, reactivity, or toxicity as defined in 40 CFR 261 Subpart C, or
 is listed waste as defined in 40 CFR 261 Subpart D.
 3.1.18 Incoming Chemical Screening Analysis – analytical methods used by the
 on-site Chemistry Laboratory to screen waste samples, pre-shipment or
 incoming shipment, for general chemistry parameters to assist with
 characterizing waste samples for waste handling.
 3.1.19 Laboratory Generated Wastes – waste related to or generated as part of
 laboratory analysis procedures.
 3.1.20 Liquid Wastes – any waste shipment profiled to contain greater than 1%
 free liquids. Also, liquid portions of analytical process residue or
 chemicals that contain minimal solid particles.
 3.1.21 Paint Filter Liquids Test “PFLT” – an EPA test used to determine the
 presence of free liquids within a solid sample. If visual inspection of a
 shipment is inconclusive for free liquids determination, a sample may be
 placed within the paint filter and monitored for liquid separation.
 3.1.22 Polychlorinated Biphenyl (PCB) – chemical substance(s) that is a biphenyl
 molecule that has been chlorinated to a varying degree.
 3.1.23 Pre-Shipment Waste Sample – waste material sent to the Clive facility for
 treatability study testing, solidification study testing, or other pre-shipment

Non-Proprietary Page 5 of 27

 waste characterization purposes as defined in Reference 2.6 and
 Attachment II-1 in Reference 2.7.
 3.1.24 Radiological Analysis – analysis for radiological content and activity
 performed on incoming waste to determine compliance with Radioactive
 Material License limits and Waste Profile Record ranges.
 3.1.25 Sample Collector (Sampler) – Sample Technician (ST) that collects
 samples, documents collection, generates COC forms, and submits
 samples to Sample Control.
 3.1.26 Sample Label- a label with sample information for identification purposes
 (i.e. sample ID, date/time of sampling, location, etc) and adhered directly
 to the sample container.
 3.1.27 Satellite Accumulation Container – a container used to store hazardous
 waste prior to disposal at the Mixed Waste facility. It must be marked and
 managed according to 40 CFR 262 requirements and regulations.
 3.1.28 Secured Location – a container, room or area that has limited access to an
 individual or department that can be used for sample storage to ensure the
 integrity and custody of the sample maintained (i.e. individuals locked
 locker, locked vehicle, locked refrigerator, or storage area with restricted
 access to authorized personnel only, etc.).
 3.1.29 Third Party Laboratory – a Utah Certified laboratory contracted with
 EnergySolutions, Clive Facility, to perform Chemical Analyses or
 Radiological Analyses.
 3.1.30 Waste Profile Record (WPR) – Information from a generator that describes
 a single waste stream. This information is provided as described in
 Reference 2.11.
 3.2 Responsibilities
 3.2.1 Lead, Laboratory, or designee, reviews all documents associated with pre-
 shipment waste sample acceptance and analysis, oversees the daily
 operations of the onsite Chemistry and Radiological Laboratories, to
 include the Sample Control area and the sampling process. Ensures that
 all personnel involved in sampling and sample management shall be
 trained and/or qualified in accordance with this procedure. Directs the
 sampling and analysis of incoming shipments and authorizes alternate
 sampling methods as needed and ensures that only qualified personnel
 within the Chemistry Laboratory implement this procedure.
 3.2.2 Sample Control, shall document receipt of all pre-shipment waste sample
 material, track sample storage, and ensure delivery of sample residues to
 appropriate areas. Oversees all incoming waste shipment evaluation for
 sampling requirements, sampling documentation preparation and the
 sampling process. Oversees sample management to include sample prep
 and processing for analysis both onsite and offsite. Monitors sample

Non-Proprietary Page 6 of 27

 processing to ensure sample storage, analysis and disposal are completed
 in a timely manner. Maintains all records associated with sample receipt,
 sample collection, sample chain-of-custody and sample disposal, ensuring
 completion of all necessary forms and the transmittal of these forms to
 Document Control for storage and maintenance.
 3.2.3 Radiation Safety Officer (RSO) or designee shall review and complete the
 radiological characterization of the Waste Profile Record associated with
 the pre-shipment waste sample, authorizes alternative sampling methods
 or waiving radiological sampling requirements for incoming waste
 shipments that do not lend themselves to sampling. The RSO may not
 waive chemical sampling requirements.
 3.2.4 Environmental Engineer, or designee, approves pre-shipment waste
 samples for shipment to the Clive Facility and contacts regulators for
 notifications or approvals when necessary, authorizes alternative sampling
 methods or waiving sampling requirements for incoming waste shipments
 that do not lend themselves to sampling, to exclude the waiving of
 radiological sampling requirements.
 3.2.5 Safety & Health Manager, or designee, shall assess chemical risks
 associated with pre-shipment waste samples.
 3.2.6 Technical Services Manager (TSM), or designee, is responsible for
 coordinating the approval, analysis requirements, and shipping of pre-
 shipment waste samples.
 3.2.7 Lead, MW Treatment is responsible for performing treatability studies
 within the laboratory and for properly storing and managing all samples
 used for treatability studies.
 3.2.8 Custody Holder is responsible for maintaining custody of a sample(s)
 within their possession.
 3.2.9 Sample Technicians collect samples from incoming shipments and assists
 in disposal of samples in accordance with this procedure. ST Shall sample
 incoming waste shipments requiring sampling after careful review of the
 Radioactive Waste Profile Record and available shipping information to
 identify potential health and safety hazards. Shall sample safely and
 compliantly, ensuring all documentation is completed and all samples and
 documentation are forwarded to the onsite Chemistry Laboratory.
 3.2.10 Shipping and Receiving Department perform sample packaging and
 labeling for outgoing shipments according to applicable regulations and
 for ensuring that required shipping papers are complete and accurate.
 3.2.11 Operations Manager, or designee, ensures waste sampling operations are
 performed compliantly and ensure waste is not managed until approved by
 the lab.
 3.3 Precautions and Limitations

Non-Proprietary Page 7 of 27

             3.3.1   General
 3.3.1.1 All personnel handling waste material shall comply with
 applicable Radiation Work Permit (RWP) requirements
 including Personal Protective Equipment (PPE).
 3.3.1.2 If there are concerns with the potential health or safety
 hazards associated with waste material being handled,
 personnel shall review the Generator Packet information
 pertaining to the waste. The Safety & Health Manager
 should be consulted as necessary when determining
 appropriate PPE and/or safe work practices.
 3.3.1.3 The steps in this procedure may only be performed by
 qualified individuals, or individuals trained on the tasks and
 activities contained in this procedure in accordance with
 Reference 2.14.
 3.3.1.4 All personnel performing tasks within the Chemistry
 Laboratory shall comply with the applicable requirements
 of reference 2.4.
 3.3.2 Pre-Shipment Waste Sample Acceptance and Tracking
 3.3.2.1 The requirements of Section 4.7 in Reference 2.11 shall be
 performed by the TSM prior to receipt of a pre-shipment
 waste sample.
 3.3.2.2 Each pre-shipment waste sample is required to have the
 following:
 • a completed Pre-Shipment Waste Sample
 Acceptance Record (CL-WM-PR-001-F5)
 completed in accordance with Reference 2.11;
 • a Uniform Low-Level Radioactive Waste Manifest;
 • a completed and signed review of the Waste Profile
 Record by the RSO, or designee; and
 • an approval provided by the Environmental
 Engineer, or designee.
 Note: The Notice to Transport (NTT) is not
 required for Pre-Shipment Waste
 Samples.
 3.3.2.3 The Director of the Division of Waste Management and
 Radiation Control shall be notified within five (5) calendar
 days of the receipt of any pre-shipment waste sample that is
 missing any of the information in 3.3.1.2.
 3.3.2.4 Pre-shipment waste samples must meet radiological and

Non-Proprietary Page 8 of 27

Sample Control Revision 2

 chemical acceptance criteria of References 2.6 and 2.7.
 3.3.2.5 Pre-shipment waste samples undergoing treatability studies
 shall be managed in accordance with 40 CFR 261.4(f).
 • Pre-shipment waste samples not managed under 40
 CFR 261.4(f) shall not exceed 15 cubic feet per
 waste stream unless authorized by the Director of
 the Division of Waste Management and Radiation
 Control.
 3.3.2.6 All waste associated with samples shall be disposed or
 returned to the generator within one year of receipt of the
 pre-shipment waste sample or treatability study sample
 shipment.
 3.3.2.7 The pre-shipment waste sample shipment will be managed
 as an incoming shipment and tracked with a TS bates
 number in accordance with Reference 2.10.
 3.3.3 Sample Control- Chain of Custody
 3.3.3.1 A sample is considered to be in an individual’s custody if it
 is:
 • In their physical possession;
 • In continuous view after possession has been
 assumed; or
 • The sample is secured or monitored by the custody
 holder so that no one can gain access to the sample
 without being detected by the custody holder.
 3.3.3.2 Each transfer of a sample must be properly documented on
 a COC form (Attachment 5.2).
 3.3.4 Sample Management
 3.3.4.1 Chemistry Laboratory personnel shall perform all sample
 preparation and analysis activities under a fume hood
 where those activities involve exposed waste. Respirators
 shall be used in addition to fume hoods when required by
 radiation work permits (RWPs) or as directed by the
 Radiation Safety department.
 3.3.4.2 When processing waste samples, qualified personnel shall
 frisk PPE and surrounding area to ensure no contamination
 is transferred out of designated areas. This survey shall be
 done in accordance to reference 2.5.
 Note: If any contamination is detected, warn
 other personnel to stay clear of the area

Non-Proprietary Page 9 of 27

Sample Control Revision 2

 and notify Radiation Safety immediately.
 3.3.4.3 The Sample Storage Refrigerator in the Sample Control
 area shall be maintained at 4°C ± 2°C. A constancy check
 shall be performed per working day using a thermometer
 maintained in accordance with reference 2.2.
 Note: In the case of catastrophic failure of the
 refrigerator or if the temperature falls
 outside the acceptable range, all samples
 contained in the refridgerator that
 require thermal preservation shall be
 disposed of and a new sample will be
 acquired.
 3.3.4.4 Satellite accumulation of RCRA waste within the
 Chemistry Laboratory shall not exceed 55-gallons of
 hazardous waste or 1-quart of acutely hazardous waste at
 any one collection point, in accordance with 40 CFR
 262.34.
 3.3.4.5 Only Utah Certified Laboratory will be contracted with
 EnergySolutions, Clive Facility, to perform chemical
 analyses or radiological analyses.
 3.3.4.6 The Laboratory Manager and Sample Control Officer shall
 utilize the BI Publisher 90-Day or 45-Day 11e.(2)
 Compliance report to ensure sample analysis analytical
 reporting may be obtained within 90-days of shipment
 arrival for LLRW and MW waste samples and within 45-
 days for 11e.(2) waste samples.
 Note: If 90Sr analysis is required with the
 radiological analysis, the required time
 period for results received is 120 days.
 3.3.4.7 Sample management shall be in accordance the Radioactive
 Material License, Waste Characterization Plan and State-
 issued Part B Permit Attachment II-1, Waste Analysis Plan,
 where applicable.
 3.3.4.8 All Satellite Accumulation Containers shall be managed in
 accordance with 40 CFR 262.34.
 Note: Satellite Accumulation Containers shall
 remain closed at all times except when
 adding or removing waste.
 3.3.5 Establishing Incoming Waste Sampling Requirements
 3.3.5.1 Process control review and database automation will be a

Non-Proprietary Page 10 of 27

 peer review of Sample Collection Report documentation.
 3.3.5.2 Sample collection and analysis may be waived if the waste
 is not amenable to sampling. Examples include: solid-
 phase metals, wood (excluding sawdust or shavings),
 concrete, brick, stone, glass, plastic, and rubber
 Note: When sampling is waived, laboratory
 personnel shall document that it was
 waived and provide a brief description of
 what the shipment contained in the
 Electronic Tracking System.
 3.3.5.3 Radiological waivers shall be approved by the RSO in
 writing in accordance with the Waste Characterization Plan
 and Radioactive Material License Condition 13G for
 shipments with Special Nuclear Material. Review of the
 radiological evaluation form within the waste profile shall
 provide guidance in expected sampling requirements per
 waste stream.
 3.3.5.4 Prior to shipment approval for management, notify the
 Lead, Laboratory of discrepancies between the waste and
 the Generator Packet information for the generator/waste-
 stream.
 3.3.5.5 Radiological and Deferred Chemical reporting and review
 is managed in accordance with reference 2.3, to include the
 updating of the Electronic Tracking System, where
 applicable.
 3.3.5.6 LLRW Waste Samples
 3.3.5.6.1 Samples sent to a Third Party Laboratory shall be
 done within established holding times for
 constituents being analyzed.
 3.3.5.6.2 LLRW shipments with free liquids and managed at
 the Mixed Waste facility shall be sampled and
 analyzed for Incoming Chemical Screening
 Parameters in accordance with Reference 2.1.
 3.3.5.6.3 The Lead, Laboratory, or designee, shall approve all
 waivers for the sampling and analysis of LLRW
 waste shipments that contain PCB’s and consist of
 the non-samplable material listed in section 3.3.5.2
 above, when these materials cannot be sampled
 reasonably or representatively.
 3.3.5.6.4 Unexpected free-liquids shall be managed in
 accordance with reference 2.12, 1% free liquid

Non-Proprietary Page 11 of 27

Sample Control Revision 2

 volume determination.
 3.3.5.6.5 For shipments that contain regulated PCBs, all
 containers shall be inspected for free standing
 liquids and managed in accordance with reference
                                         2.7, Attachment II-1-10.1.
                  3.3.5.7          11e.(2) Waste Samples
 3.3.5.7.1 11e.(2) samples for Deferred Chemical Analysis are
 to be sent to a Third Party Laboratory within 24
 hours of being sampled.
 3.3.5.7.2 PFLT samples shall be collected in accordance with
 reference 2.9, condition 10.6.
 3.3.5.7.3 Unexpected free-liquids shall be managed in
 accordance with reference 2.12, 1% free liquid
 volume determination
 3.3.5.8 Mixed Waste Samples
 3.3.5.8.1 Samples shall be collected from highway shipments
 within seven calendar days of arrival and rail
 shipments within ten calendar days of arrival.
 3.3.5.8.2 Samples sent to a Third Party Laboratory shall be
 done within established holding times for
 constituents being analyzed or within 2 working
 days of being sampled.
 3.3.5.8.3 When it is deemed that a waste shipment doesn’t
 lend itself to sampling, the Environmental Engineer,
 or the Lead, Laboratory may waive the analysis.
 3.3.5.8.3.1 Alternative sampling methods may
 be approved by the Environmental
 Engineer, or the Lead, Laboratory.
 3.3.5.8.4 Treatment residues from stabilization/solidification,
 oxidation, neutralization, amalgamation, or other
 similar treatment technologies shall NOT be
 waived.
 3.3.5.8.5 The Lead, Laboratory, or designee, shall approve all
 waivers for the sampling and analysis of mixed
 waste shipments that contain PCB’s and consist of
 the non-samplable material listed in section 3.3.5.2
 above, when these materials cannot be sampled
 reasonably or representatively.
 3.3.5.8.6 For shipments that contain regulated PCBs, all
 containers shall be inspected for free standing

Non-Proprietary Page 12 of 27

 liquids and managed in accordance with reference
                                           2.7, Attachment II-1-10; Attachment II-1-10.1.
 3.4 Records
 3.4.1 All documentation shall be transmitted to Document Control for storage
 and maintenance in accordance with Reference 2.15.

4 REQUIREMENTS AND GUIDANCE
 4.1 Pre-Shipment Waste Sample Acceptance and Tracking
 4.1.1 Pre-Shipment Waste Sample Acceptance
 4.1.1.1 The Shipping and Receiving Coordinator shall notify
 Sample Control after acceptance of a pre-shipment waste
 sample, as required in Reference 2.6.
 4.1.1.2 Specific management required of the sample will be
 provided by the Technical Services Manager.
 4.1.1.3 Specific analytical requirements of the sample will be
 provided by the Environmental Engineer.
 4.1.1.4 At a minimum, on-site chemical (as required) and
 radiological analyses will be performed on all pre-shipment
 samples tracked with TS bates numbers.
 4.1.1.5 Upon completion of the processes (including verification of
 successful treatment formula development) and/or
 analytical results received, any unused portions of pre-
 shipment waste samples will be returned to the original
 shipping container.
 4.1.2 Pre-Shipment Waste and Treatability Study Sample Storage and/or
 Disposal
 4.1.2.1 At least quarterly, the Sample Control Officer, or designee,
 shall conduct a surveillance, in accordance with Reference
 2.13, on the pre-shipment and treatability study waste
 samples in storage in the laboratory area (including the
 storage sea vans). The surveillance shall include a list of
 all pre-shipment and treatability study waste samples in
 inventory, the date each sample was received at Clive, and
 the intended disposition of each sample.
 Note: All samples shall be disposed or returned
 to the generator within one year of
 acceptance of the shipment.
 4.1.2.2 The Environmental Engineer will be provided a copy of the
 surveillance.

Non-Proprietary Page 13 of 27

 4.1.2.3 All pre-shipment and treatability study waste samples, and
 residues generated from processing these samples will be
 managed in one of the following ways:
 • combined with waste from the appropriate generator
 waste stream,
 • managed as EnergySolutions Generated Waste in
 accordance with Reference 2.8, or
 • returned to the generator.
 Note: The initial acceptance date of the Pre-
 Shipment Sample or the waste associated
 with the treatability study sample must be
 used for tracking the one year disposal
 requirement.
 4.1.2.4 The off-site laboratory shall be responsible for disposal of
 all un-used sample portions and analytical residues in
 accordance with their procedures, unless otherwise agreed
 upon.
 4.2 Sample Control- Chain of Custody
 4.2.1 Sample Collection
 4.2.1.1 All sample(s) shall be collected by trained/qualified
 personnel in accordance with the applicable procedures
 under which the sampling is being performed.
 4.2.1.2 The sample collector (sampler) shall fill out the sample
 label or mark the sample container or collection bag to
 identify each sample, with the following information, at
 minimum:
 • The generator’s number
 • The Bates or shipment number
 • Container number or location of sample collection
 • Date and time of collection
 • Initials of person collecting the sample
 Note: If the sample is a personnel or
 environmental monitoring sample or an
 extraordinary sample, generator, bates
 number and/or container information
 may not be associated with the sample
 taken. Ensure that a brief description of
 the sample and location or person the

Non-Proprietary Page 14 of 27

Sample Control Revision 2

 sample is associated with is included with
 the date and time of collection and the
 initials of the person collecting the
 sample.
 4.2.1.3 If the sampler must leave the sample unattended prior to
 being relinquished to the laboratory, the sampler shall
 ensure the integrity of the sample(s) is maintained by
 applying custody seals in such a manner as to secure the
 opening of the outermost sample container, or place the
 samples in a secured location.
 4.2.2 Transfer Sample to Sample Control
 4.2.2.1 The sample collector shall deliver the sample(s) to Sample
 Control personnel and complete the COC form for
 relinquishment to the Laboratory.
 4.2.2.2 The COC form shall include the following, at minimum:
 • Date COC generated
 • Name of custody holder (or sample team leader)
 • Sampling team
 • Field log book page number(s) (when applicable)
 • Remarks (Sample analysis information)
 • Sample ID (Bates number or other unique ID
 number)
 • Description (Generator and waste stream number or
 brief sample description)
 • Sample collection date(s) and time(s)
 Note: Electronic COC forms generated by the
 COC Database contain an integrated
 barcode as a designated Station/Location
 for laboratory convenience in processing.
 4.2.2.3 Laboratory personnel shall inspect the sample(s) for proper
 container, labeling and that all necessary documentation is
 present with the sample(s), prior to accepting receipt of the
 sample(s) and signing the COC form.
 4.2.3 Sample Processing
 4.2.3.1 Once obtaining custody of sample, the sample(s) shall be
 stored.
 4.2.3.2 While awaiting preparation and transferring, the sample or

Non-Proprietary Page 15 of 27

 group of samples shall be placed in a secured location.
 4.2.3.3 All custody transfers from Sample Control area to
 analytical personnel shall be documented appropriately on
 the COC form.
 4.2.3.4 If a transfer COC form is used for a sample(s) or a
 portion(s) of a sample specific to the analysis required or
 the receiving personnel or facility, the following
 information must also be transferred to the new COC form:
 • All original sample information noted on the
 original COC form, to include: sampling team,
 sample ID, sample description, sample date and
 time.
 • The number of the original COC form transferred
 from.
 4.2.3.5 When sample(s) or a portion(s) of a sample is transferred to
 another COC form, to include off site facilities, the
 following information must be noted on the original COC
 form:
 • The number of the destination COC form.
 • The initials of the person performing the transfer
 (the custody holder at the time).
 • The date and time of the transfer.
 Note: Off-site facilities shall maintain proper
 custody of the sample(s) within their
 control according to their procedures and
 shall provide this documentation if
 requested by EnergySolutions, Clive
 Facility.
 4.2.3.6 While awaiting analysis, the sample or group of samples
 shall be placed in a secured location.
 4.2.3.7 If the sample(s) is completely consumed in the analytical
 process, this disposition shall be noted on the COC form
 and the form shall be returned to Sample Control for
 documentation.
 4.2.4 Sample Return — Process for Disposal
 4.2.4.1 Upon completion of all analytical work, the analyst
 (custody holder) shall relinquish any remaining sample(s)
 back to personnel in the Sample Control area.
 Note: Sample(s) sent to off-site facilities will not

Non-Proprietary Page 16 of 27

 be returned, unless EnergySolutions
 personnel specifically requests return or
 the off-site facility requires return of the
 sample due to high contamination.
 4.2.4.2 Samples shall be stored within the Sample Control area,
 until it is determined that no other analytical work is
 required.
 4.2.4.3 Once the remaining sample(s) are processed into
 designated waste containers for disposal at the appropriate
 on-site facility, the date and time disposed and who
 disposed of it shall be noted on the COC form and the form
 returned to Sample Control.
 4.2.4.4 If COC forms involve samples from incoming waste
 shipment sampling, the Sample Control Officer shall
 update all sample COC information in the Electronic
 Tracking System prior to transmitting to Document Control
 for storage and maintenance.
 4.3 Sample Management
 4.3.1 Sample Collection
 4.3.1.1 Sample Technicians shall sample incoming waste in
 accordance with reference 2.12, and relinquish all samples
 to Laboratory personnel, ensuring that the samples are
 clearly labeled.
 4.3.1.2 Qualified personnel shall perform dose rate measurements
 on all waste samples. The dose rate shall be recorded on
 the sample container if it is above 2 mrem/hr on contact.
 4.3.2 Sample Compositing and Containerizing for Analysis
 4.3.2.1 Sample aliquots that require compositing shall be done by
 mixing the aliquots in a stainless steel, glass, or brass bowl
 using a stainless steel, glass, or brass mixing device.
 4.3.2.2 After mixing has been completed, sub-samples of the
 composited sample shall be taken from the mixture and
 transferred into the appropriate container(s) size and type as
 specified by the applicable analytical method(s) required.
 4.3.2.3 Additional requirements for samples requiring volatile
 analysis:

Non-Proprietary Page 17 of 27

Sample Control Revision 2

 4.3.2.3.1 The volatile sub-sample(s) shall be transferred first
 into glass sample container(s), within five minutes
 of the completion of the compositing process.
 4.3.2.3.2 The volatile sub-sample shall be taken from the
 center of the waste mixture.
 4.3.2.3.3 The volatile sub-sample container(s) shall be
 immediately capped off, minimizing the amount of
 headspace in the container.
 4.3.3 Sample Transferring for Analysis
 4.3.3.1 Once the sample is placed in the appropriate sample
 container, if applicable, the sample shall be stored in
 accordance with Section 4.3.4 of this procedure and/or
 transferred to the personnel responsible for analysis (both
 on-site and off-site).
 Note: Samples shall be transferred from the
 original sample collection Chain of
 Custody (COC) to a COC specifying the
 destination and required analysis (both
 on-site and off-site analysis).
 4.3.3.2 When samples for on-site analysis have been processed and
 transferred accordingly, the appropriate analytical
 personnel shall be notified that the samples are available
 for analysis.
 Note: Samples for Radiological Analysis are
 collected and stored in the Sample
 Control Area until a complete COC is
 established or approximately a week
 worth of samples to assist in sample
 analysis batching. Sample Control shall
 print the BI Publisher 90-Day or 45-Day
 11e.(2) Compliance report to monitor
 sample holding times to ensure samples
 are being processed and transferred in a
 timely manner.
 4.3.3.3 When samples for off-site analysis have been processed
 and transferred accordingly, notify the Shipping and
 Receiving Department for shipment off-site to the
 appropriate Third Party Laboratory for analysis.
 4.3.4 Sample Storage
 4.3.4.1 While awaiting transfer to on-site personnel for analysis
 (i.e. Immediate Chemical Screening Analysis, Radiological

Non-Proprietary Page 18 of 27

 Analysis, Moisture Analysis, etc.) samples may be stored in
 the Sample Control Room.
 4.3.4.2 Samples awaiting compositing and packaging for transfer
 and shipment off-site to a Third Party Laboratory for
 Deferred Chemical (DC) Analysis, shall be stored in the
 Sample Storage Refrigerator in the Sample Control area,
 unless the compositing and packaging of the sample is to
 take place immediately upon receipt in the Laboratory.
 4.3.4.3 Samples with a dose rate of 2 mrem/hr or greater upon
 contact shall not be stored long term in the Sample Control
 Room. These samples shall be prepared and processed for
 transferring to designated department(s), onsite or offsite,
 for pending analyses as soon as possible. If long term
 storage is required, the sample shall be adequately shielded
 or moved to the Sample Control Storage Area, to ensure
 dose rate exposure to personnel is minimized.
 4.3.4.4 Pre-Shipment Samples shall be stored in a secure area
 while awaiting processing and/or analysis. These samples
 may be stored in the Sample Control Room, or the main
 Chemistry Laboratory, unless dose rates of 2 mrem/hr or
 greater require storage in the Sample Control Storage Area.
 4.3.4.5 Samples that no longer require analysis shall be disposed of
 as outlined in Section 4.3.5, or may be stored in the Sample
 Control Storage Area pending offsite results or other
 verification, as necessary.
 4.3.4.6 Samples that have completed processing and/or analysis
 and are awaiting disposal shall be segregated into 11e.(2),
 LLRW and Mixed Waste storage containers.
 Note: Sample Control shall ensure that Mixed
 Waste for direct disposal is segregated
 from Mixed Waste for treatment.
 4.3.5 Disposal of samples
 4.3.5.1 At least monthly, dispose samples that are no longer needed
 for analysis.
 4.3.5.2 Document disposal of samples in the Electronic Tracking
 System with the input of the date disposed.
 4.3.5.3 Dispose samples either with the original waste stream or
 accumulate by waste type (LLRW, 11e.(2), and Mixed
 Waste) as an EnergySolutions waste stream for
 management and disposal as site generated waste.

Non-Proprietary Page 19 of 27

 4.3.6 Chemistry Laboratory Generated Wastes
 4.3.6.1 Manage wastes that result from the Immediate Chemical
 Screening Analysis process (or any other chemical
 analysis) as Laboratory Generated Waste.
 Note: 11e.(2) waste samples do not require
 onsite chemical analyses that would
 require management as laboratory
 generated wastes.
 4.3.7 Liquid waste associated with Laboratory Generated Wastes:
 4.3.7.1 Liquid residue from LLRW and Mixed Waste sample
 analysis may be decanted into a liquid Satellite
 Accumulation Container.
 4.3.7.2 Other liquid laboratory wastes (i.e. expired chemicals,
 expired reagents, etc.) shall be disposed of in a liquid
 Satellite Accumulation Container if they are determined to
 possess hazardous characteristics.
 4.3.8 Solid waste associated with Laboratory Generated Wastes
 4.3.8.1 The solid residue from LLRW and Mixed Waste sample
 analyses shall be collected and visually verified that it does
 not contain free liquids. If free liquids are present, the
 waste shall be solidified prior to disposal into the
 appropriate Satellite Accumulation Container.
 Note: A Paint Filter Liquid Test (PFLT)
 Analysis shall be performed according to
 reference 2.1 if the visual inspection does
 not give adequate indication of the liquid
 content.
 4.3.8.2 Other solid laboratory wastes shall be disposed of into the
 Satellite Accumulation Container if they are determined to
 possess hazardous constituents.
 4.3.8.3 Chemistry Laboratory Generated Waste also includes any
 Contact Waste associated with the processes of sample
 management within the lab and shall be collected in the
 appropriate Satellite Accumulation Container.
 4.3.8.4 Under the direction of Mixed Waste Operations, a Sample
 Technician may dispose of Laboratory Generated Waste
 accumulation containers as site-generated waste.

Non-Proprietary Page 20 of 27

 4.3.9 Cleaning laboratory equipment associated with sample preparation and
 analysis of RCRA hazardous waste.
 4.3.9.1 Laboratory equipment that is used to prepare and analyze
 RCRA hazardous waste samples shall be wiped visibly
 clean using an appropriate cleaner prior to placing in the
 laboratory sink.
 4.3.9.2 If equipment is not able to be wiped visibly clean, it will be
 placed in a satellite accumulation container for additional
 cleaning. Cleaning residues will be sent to Mixed Waste as
 hazardous waste for management in accordance with
 Reference 2.8.
 Note: Equipment used for acutely hazardous
 waste shall be triple rinsed with a solvent
 capable of removing residue. The solvent
 rinse then must be managed as acutely
 hazardous waste.

 4.4 Establishing Incoming Waste Sampling Requirements
 4.4.1 As incoming waste shipments are ‘accepted’, ensure appropriate samples
 are assigned to each shipment.
 4.4.1.1 The Electronic Tracking System automatically assigns
 Entry Numbers to shipments and assigns sampling in
 accordance with the frequency requirements of the licenses
 and permits see Attachment 5.3, Sample Collection
 Frequency for Incoming Shipments.
 4.4.1.2 Once a ‘type’ of sampling has been required of a shipment,
 the Electronic Tracking System populates the required
 samples and assigns the next numerical “Sample ID”
 accordingly.
 Note: A shipment with a truck and a pup is
 considered to be two shipments for the
 purposes of sampling and analysis.
 4.4.2 Review the Electronic Tracking System for any sampling automation
 discrepancies.
 4.4.3 For shipments with additional sampling requirements, provide descriptive
 notes in the Electronic Tracking System and populate the required samples
 manually. Ensure that adequate direction is provided for Sample Control
 and for the Sample Technicians.
 4.4.4 Once all sampling requirements per shipment have been assigned, print
 sample container labels and CL-LB-PR-003-F1, Sample Collection Report

Non-Proprietary Page 21 of 27

 (See example in Attachment 5.1).
 Note: Sample Collection Report document may be used with
 other samples taken on Clive facility, to include ECW
 waste, post-treatment, VTD processed material, etc.
 These documents will be for record keeping (not
 compliance) purposes and managed by Sample Control
 accordingly.
 4.4.5 Make printed documents, sampling containers, and equipment available to
 the Sample Technicians.
 4.4.6 Assure that sampling has been performed in accordance with reference
 2.12.
 4.4.7 Place all samples received by Sample Control in designated areas.
 4.4.7.1 Transfer samples for Fingerprint Analysis, including PFLT
 Analysis, directly to a Chemistry Laboratory Technician for
 immediate analysis.
 4.4.7.2 Store and process accordingly any samples that do not
 require immediate analysis.
 4.4.8 Input the sampling information into the Electronic Tracking System and
 approve the shipment for management as soon as practical.
 4.4.9 Track the onsite Fingerprint and ensure that the tests are performed in a
 timely manner. As the analytical data is reported, update the Electronic
 Tracking System where applicable.

 Executive Summary
 Upon U.S. Department of Energy request, the West Area Tank Treatment (WATT) program is preparing
 the Hanford Site 200 West Area to support retrieval, pretreatment, and subsequent shipment of pretreated
 waste for offsite treatment and disposal as a solidified or stabilized waste form. Two areas of technology
 maturation need have been identified in support of the WATT program: (1) mitigation of solids
 accumulation in double-shell tanks, and (2) removal and/or destruction of Land Disposal Restriction
 (LDR) organics potentially present in the waste.

 One approach to removing LDR organics is the application of the vacuum thermal desorption (VTD)
 process. In fiscal year 2025, Pacific Northwest National Laboratory (PNNL) supported a VTD unit
 demonstration at EnergySolutions (ES) by providing LDR inorganic spike chemicals to ES, receiving a
 post-VTD product, immobilizing it in a grout waste form, and performing Toxicity Characteristic
 Leaching Procedure (TCLP; EPA SW-846 Method 1311) testing to assess the behavior of LDR inorganic
 compounds that are present in the VTD product and waste forms, along with organics.

 The VTD product PNNL obtained from ES was stabilized using two strategies: 1) VTD product was
 treated using reductants according to the recipe provided by ES and named VTD-PT, and 2) the VTD
 product was treated, like VTD-PT, and then solidified per the ES recipe, and named VTD-GS. The
 samples were cured for 28 days. After this time, the VTD-solids, cured VTD-PT, and cured VTD-GS
 were tested according to EPA Method 1311. The two treated samples, VTD-PT and VTD-GS, both
 produced TCLP leachates that met the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency’s universal treatment
 standards (UTS) for non-wastewaters for all the LDR inorganics included in the test. The untreated
 VTD-solids met the UTS limits for all LDR inorganics except for Cr, which exceeded the UTS limit,
 showing that treatment of the VTD-solids is required for disposal.

Contents vi

 1.0 Introduction
 1.1 Background
 Approximately 55 million gallons of radioactive and hazardous waste are stored in underground tanks at
 the U.S. Department of Energy’s (DOE) Hanford Site in southeastern Washington State (Rodgers 2025).
 Hanford Tank Waste Operations and Closure (H2C) has initiated the West Area Tank Treatment (WATT)
 program to prepare the 200 West Area (200W) for waste retrieval, pretreatment and subsequent shipment
 of liquid pretreated tank waste (PTW) for offsite treatment and disposal as a solidified/stabilized waste
 form. The aim of the WATT program is retrieval and offsite disposal of the PTW generated from 24
 single-shell tanks in 200W by December 31, 2040 (Asmussen et al. 2025). The West Area Waste Feed
 Delivery Support project is part of the WATT Program and provides management, regulatory (non-permit
 related), engineering, and technical maturation support. This project allows delivery of PTW to an offsite
 commercial vendor in accordance with both the transportation and waste acceptance requirements for
 final treatment and disposal as a cementitious waste form.

 Two areas of technology maturation have been initially identified in support of the WATT mission:
 (1) mitigation of solids accumulation in SY Farm double-shell tanks and (2) removal and/or destruction of
 Land Disposal Restriction (LDR) organics potentially present in the PTW. This report documents support
 by Pacific Northwest National Laboratory (PNNL) of the vacuum thermal desorption (VTD) unit
 demonstration at EnergySolutions (ES)1, which addresses the second area of the technology maturation
 plan (Asmussen et al. 2025).

 The VTD treatment process uses additives to absorb the liquid PTW. The solids are then placed into the
 VTD system and indirectly heated under an inert atmosphere to separate volatile compounds from the
 solidified waste. The volatiles (organics and inorganics) are then condensed in the off-gas system. The
 remainder becomes a solid product, referred to in this report as the VTD product. A simulated Hanford
 tank waste was created by ES and TD*X and spiked with several organic and inorganic chemicals at
 levels predicted to bound any Hanford tank waste concentration (Gray et. al., 2025) which was
 subsequently treated by the VTD system. PNNL supported the VTD demonstration by providing
 inorganic chemicals to ES, receiving the resulting product after the VTD process, applying a treatment
 method prescribed by ES, and performing Toxicity Characteristic Leaching Procedure (TCLP; EPA SW-
 846 Method 1311) non-volatile testing on the samples.

 1.2 Objectives
 The objectives of this study were to:
  Obtain VTD product from ES.
  Prepare treated samples from the VTD product at laboratory scale.
  Perform EPA Method 1311 testing on the resulting samples.

 1
 VTD is an EPA recognized technology. See 40 CFR 268.42 and 40 CFR 268.44(o).

 1.4 Quality Assurance
 This work was supported by H2C under contract 90685-024 and conducted at PNNL as part of project
 number 85440, Waste Feed Delivery Support. This work was performed in accordance with the PNNL
 Nuclear Quality Assurance Program (NQAP) and associated quality assurance procedures. The NQAP
 complies with the DOE Order 414.1D, Quality Assurance, and 10 CFR 830, Subpart A, Quality
 Assurance Requirements. The NQAP uses NQA-1-2012, Quality Assurance Requirements for Nuclear
 Facility Applications, as its consensus standard and NQA-1-2012, Subpart 4.2.1, as the basis for its
 graded approach to quality. The NQAP works in conjunction with PNNL’s laboratory-level Quality
 Management Program, which is based on the requirements defined in DOE Order 414.1D and
 10 CFR 830, Subpart A. This work was graded and performed at technology readiness level (TRL) 4.

 The work performed at PNNL to support this report complies with the relevant NQAP QA Program
 (NQAP-2012) for this grading level, implementing procedures (IPs) for this TRL per NQAP-IP-0208, and
 any additional controls specified. Oversight of this work was on an as-planned basis in accordance with
 NQAP-IP-1801, Surveillances and Audits, or NQAP-IP-0203, Management Assessments.

Introduction 2

 2.0 Methodology
 This section describes the experimental methods, procedures, and standards used in this work. The
 treatment and solidification approach used is bound by a non-disclosure agreement.

 2.1 LDR Inorganic Material Preparation at ES
 PNNL procured and supplied LDR inorganic chemicals to ES along with batch sheets to prepare each
 VTD test drum (alternatively referred to as a batch) per the target solution concentrations presented in
 Table 2.1. The target concentrations were 3× higher than the maximum concentrations in the projected
 200W feed vector to study an upper bound. Beryllium was excluded from the LDR inorganics tested in
 this study due to work controls at both PNNL and ES limiting its use at concentrations required to attain
 meaningful data.

 The inorganic spikes were added to a PTW simulant (at 5.6 M Na) with the compositions presented in
 Table 2.2. Four 30-gallon mixtures were prepared in individual 55-gallon drums for solidification; each
 drum used 22.5 gallons of deionized water to prepare the PTW simulant. Table 2.3 presents the final
 weight of simulant components per drum. The drummed simulant was transferred to a mixer and blended
 with an additive prior to the addition of chemical spikes. The inorganics were added to a single drum
 directly before the VTD treatment to reduce potential handling exposures. The contents of the spiked
 drum were blended with the unspiked PTW simulant and solids in the VTD system feed chamber.

 Finally, each drum was emptied into the VTD feed hopper and was subjected to the VTD process. The
 final VTD treated residue (named VTD product in this document) was sampled and sent to PNNL for
 EPA Method 1311 testing.

Methodology 4

 2.2 Sample Fabrication at PNNL
 Upon receipt of the VTD product, PNNL prepared the following samples for TCLP testing:
  VTD product without prior treatment (VTD)
  VTD product treated (VTD-PT)
  VTD product treated and then solidified (VTD-GS)

 The ES treatment is designed to stabilize heavy metals present in the VTD product using series of
 successive reducing and stabilizing chemical reactions. Upon immediate preparation of VTD-PT and
 VTD-GS, both final products resembled brown dirt and were placed into molds and cured for 28 days
 before TCLP testing (Peterson et al. 2025).

      2.3 EPA Method 1311
 The TCLP (EPA SW-846 Method 1311) was performed on the VTD, VTD-PT, and VTD-GS samples.
 The methodology was adjusted from the original TCLP method in the following ways, which was
 determined to produce equivalent results on similar samples elsewhere (Reiser et al. 2025):
 1. The amount of sample needed to perform the test was reduced from 100 to 15 g. The amount of
 extraction fluid also decreased to maintain a solids to liquid ratio of 1g per 20ml.
 2. The maximum particle size used in the test was reduced from <10 to <5 mm. In addition to this, a
 minimum particle size of >250 μm was used to limit the amount of smaller particles, which
 would have otherwise increased the variance of the particle size distribution.
 3. Instead of using the 0.7-μm borosilicate glass fiber filters specified in the original procedure,
 0.45-μm polytetrafluorethylene (PTFE) filters housed in polypropylene (PP) casing were used.

 Per the TCLP method, prior to testing, a pre-test was performed to identify the extraction fluid for testing
 on the VTD product and cementitious products by adding 5.0 g of the sample with a maximum particle
 size of <1 mm to 96.5 mL of double-deionized water (DDI, ASTM D1193 Type 1 water) and stirring for
 5 minutes. As per the TCLP method, all three types of samples were acidified with 3.5 mL of 1 M HCl
 because their pH was greater than 5. Then the system was heated to 50 °C for 10 minutes, the solution
 was allowed to cool, and the pH was measured again. The VTD and VTD-PT recorded a pH > 5 after the
 acid addition, resulting in the use of the Extraction Fluid 2 [5.7 mL glacial acetic acid (CH3COOH)
 diluted to 1 L with a final pH of 2.88 ± 0.05]. However, the VTD-GS pH results were <5, resulting in the
 use of the Extraction Fluid 1 [5.7 mL glacial acetic acid (CH3COOH), 63.5 mL of 1 M NaOH diluted to 1
 L with a final pH of 4.93 ± 0.05]. These results were verified by performing the pre-tests in triplicate.

 For TCLP testing, 28-day cured samples were prepared as described above. A 15-g portion of sample
 (VTD, VTP-PT, and VTP-GS) and 300 mL of corresponding extraction fluid were added into a 500-mL
 PP vessel. The vessels were then put into a rotary device and tumbled with a rotational speed of
 30 ± 2 rpm for 18 ± 2 hours at 23 ± 2 °C. At the end of the test, the vessels were removed, and the
 extraction fluid was filtered through a 0.45-μm PTFE syringe filter. For one randomly selected sample
 (matrix spike, VTD-MS) and blank (blank spike), solutions were spiked with an internal standard to
 assess retrieval percentages were within the confidence interval of 75% to 115% for each analyte of
 interest and to exclude potential interferences from the extraction fluid or spiking solution. The solutions
 were preserved with HNO3 and refrigerated until analyzed. TCLP tests were performed in triplicate for
 each solid sample (i.e., VTD, VTD-PT, and VTD-GS) to determine averages and standard deviations.

Methodology 5

 Each TCLP test was sampled to measure the different analytes in the leachate solution according to
 governing SW-846 procedures (Table 2.4). The resulting concentrations were used to calculate leachate
 concentrations to compare against universal treatment standards (UTS) limits for non-wastewater and
 corresponding retention factors.

 Retention factors were calculated using Eq. (1) to quantify and compare LDR inorganic retention in the
 different solids:

 𝑚𝑖,𝑇𝐶𝐿𝑃,𝐿 𝐶𝑖,𝑇𝐶𝐿𝑃 ∙ 𝑉𝑇𝐶𝐿𝑃
 𝑅𝐹𝑖 1 1 (1)
 𝑚𝑖,𝑇𝐶𝐿𝑃,𝑆 𝐶𝑖,𝑆𝑜𝑙𝑖𝑑 ∙ 𝑚𝑇𝐶𝐿𝑃,𝑆

 where:
 mi,TCLP,L is the mass of species i in the EPA Method 1311 leachate, in mg,
 mi,TCLP,S is the mass of species i in the solids used in the EPA Method 1311 test, in mg,
 Ci,TCLP is the concentration of species i in the leachate, in mgꞏL-1,
 VTCLP is the volume of the extractant fluid in the test, in L,
 Ci,Solid is the concentration of species i in the solid sample, mgꞏkg-1, and
 mTCLP,S is the initial total mass of the solids used in the EPA Method 1311 test, in kg.

Methodology 6

 3.0 Results and Discussion
 3.1 VTD In-Laboratory Treatment
 The as-received VTD product was very heterogeneous (Figure 3.1), but in general it was processed in its
 entirety as much as possible. Large pieces that were found during weighing of solids were not included in
 the laboratory treatment for safety reasons, as these could get stuck in the planetary mixer, damage the
 device, or even be ejected from the bowl during mixing.

 Figure 3.1. Example of large pieces found in VTD solids. These pieces were removed prior to the
 treatment.

 The treatment method applied to produce the VTD-PT sample (Figure 3.2) resulted in very dry,
 gravel-like material. The product was packed into 2-in. (inner diameter) × 4-in. (high) polycarbonate
 molds, sealed, and placed into a curing chamber for 28 days. After this time, the VTD-PT sample returned
 to the original shade of red, barely maintained its shape, and crumbed upon light pressure, see Figure 3.3.

 Figure 3.2. VTD-PT sample preparation: (a) VTD solids and water, (b) sample after reagent A, (c) sample
 after reagent B, (d) sample after reagent C, and (e) final product.

Results and Discussion 7

 Figure 3.3. (a) VTD-PT taken out of polycarbonate mold after 28 days, (b) cross-section of broken
 sample.

 The preparation of VTD-GS (Figure 3.4) resulted in similarly dry, gravel-like material, necessitating an
 extra addition of DDI during addition of the solidification additive. The solidification additive and DDI
 water were added in two batches to ensure proper homogenization and to observe any potential changes
 induced by each addition. The final fresh VTD-GS appeared wetter than VTD-PT with better formability
 when added to the 2-in (inner diameter) × 4-in. (high) polycarbonate molds. After 28 days, the samples
 cured into sound monoliths (Figure 3.5a), one of which was broken apart for testing. Inside the broken
 monolith, light blue pieces were observed, which quickly turned to light yellow (Figure 3.5c) and were
 likely evidence of oxidization of Cr.

Results and Discussion 8

 Figure 3.4. TD-GS sample preparation: (a) VTD product and water, (b) sample after reagent A (c) sample
 after reagent B, (d) sample after reagent C, (e) first addition of solidification additive and
 DDI, (f) second addition of solidification additive and DDI, and (g) final product.

 Figure 3.5. (a) VTD-GS monolith upon demolding from polycarbonate mold, (b) VTD-GS monolith
 cross-section, (c) close-up view showing different colored grains inside the sample.

 3.2 EPA Method 1311 Testing
 Table 3.1 presents the final measured concentrations of the LDR inorganics in the TCLP leachates. Based
 on the results of pre-testing, Extraction Fluid 1 was used for VTD-GS and Extraction Fluid 2 was used for
 VTD and VTD-PT for EPA Method 1311 testing. For the VTD product, leachate concentrations for all
 elements were below the UTS non-wastewater limits, with the exception of Cr, which was higher than its
 corresponding limit by an order of magnitude. Se was the next highest release concentration from the

Results and Discussion 9

 VTD product. It is important to note that the concentration of Hg in VTD product leachate was also very
 low, suggesting the process may have removed a large fraction of the Hg from the VTD product, due to
 volatilization.

 The measured concentrations of TCLP leachates obtained from VTD-PT and VTD-GS confirm that the
 use of reductants suppresses Cr release and, in both cases, the measured values are lower than the UTS
 non-wastewater limit. The leachate concentrations for As, Hg and Se were an order of magnitude lower
 than the concentrations in the un-treated VTD product. The addition of the solidification additive in the
 VTD-GS resulted in higher concentrations of Ba in the TCLP leachate, likely due to Ba presence in the
 solidification additive, however the Ba in solution was still well below the UTS limit, see Table 3.1.
 Retention factors were also calculated and are presented in Table 3.2. Retention factors can be used to
 assess the success of a treatment method and in support of processing calculations to ensure LDR non-
 wastewater concentration-based treatment standards can be met. In the majority of cases, the retention
 factor remained reasonably high, with the most significant improvement between the VTD product and
 VTD-PT/GS for Cr. The retention factor of Ba was negative for the VTD-GS and is assumed to be caused
 by the release of Ba from the solidification additive. The retention factor for Se was comparable across all
 three types of samples, suggesting that Se retention is limited and not impacted by the reduction process.
 Finally, when comparing the VTD-PT and VTD-GS waste forms, the solidification addition resulted in a
 more mechanically robust waste form, but its presence did not significantly impact TCLP performance.
 Most of the decrease in measured TCLP leachate concentrations was due to the dilution of the initial LDR
 inorganics spike present in the waste form by the solidification addition rather than any stabilizing effects.
 This observation is supported by the fact that the RF values for VTD-PT and VTD-GS were similar in
 Table 3.2.

Results and Discussion 12

 4.0 Summary
 PNNL supported the EnergySolutions (ES)-led demonstration of the Vacuum Thermal Desorption (VTD)
 process of simulated pretreated waste spiked with Land Disposal Restriction (LDR) inorganics at 3 times
 more than the anticipated concentrations in the West Area Tank Treatment mission, except for LDR
 inorganic Be that was not evaluated in this testing. To support this demonstration, PNNL conducted EPA
 SW-846 Method 1311 testing (also referred to as the TCLP test) on: (1) VTD product without treatment
 (VTD), (2) treated VTD product (VTD-PT), and (3) treated and solidified VTD product (VTD-GS). The
 outcome was:
 1. The un-treated VTD product resulted in a dry, loose powder material that passed the Universal
 Treatment Standards (UTS) limits for all LDR inorganics except for Cr, by EPA Method 1311
 testing.
 2. Performing the ES treatment process on the VTD product (VTD-PT) resulted in a loose,
 gravel-like waste form, with no significant mechanical integrity. This waste form passed the UTS
 limits for all LDR inorganics. Cr, Ba, and Hg were impacted most by the reduction process used
 and decreased concentration in the TCLP liquid compared with the un-treated VTD product.
 3. Solidifying the treated VTD product with an additive (VTD-GS) increased mechanical strength,
 resulting in a monolithic waste form that passed the UTS limits for all LDR inorganics. The
 additive did not improve retention of LDR inorganics beyond product dilution, relative to the
 VTD-PT sample.
